to approach the managing director of thie firm. fle did so in a respectful manner. He was quietly intormed that, se far as he was concerned, they could iseanaae their business without the interference of a third party, and that th .e were al waos willing to dc ii fairly with their employ di. He suggested to the men that thee should sign a petition to the firm. They did so, with the same result. He (the epeaker) salted them respect- fully to give ear to the petition, and the result was that each man received his notice, supplemented be a no"e saying that if they were desirous ot complying with the condition of employment as existing up to date thy might apply to the manager, Mr. Dale, who would re- engage them providing there was a vacancy to fill. The men clime out, and he contended they were in their right in asking to be Consi irred at this juncture. These men were asking for nothing that was wrong ; they were simply asking far conditions which were in r eiet • ence at Port Sunlight. (Applause.) He conaidered I at in dealing with a firm tmey oueht always to he ei regard to the conditions of work in a r induste (Hear, hear). These men at Meaars Ce retield's cud not want to as for any exorbitant rate of wages or cen- ditione of employment hut were seeking to obtain con- ditions similar to those enjoyed by the engine men and boilermeci employed by Messrs Lever Bros. at Port Sunliebt. (Beer, )'es+) There were the condition,' (fielding up a sheet ot paper) which had been sent him by Mr Lever himself. (Applauee). Those conditions kad been in existence at Port Suulignt for seven years. Messrs Lever Bros had always been swilling to meet the Trades Union for the discussion of any qneition which might be laid before them. The men at Messrs Crosfielde desired similar conditions to those at Port Sun- light. The position at the present time was this. The men in charge of the boilers were receiving aid per hour, whilst at Port Sunlight the men received sixpence per hour and OOP and a quarter ti ne for any time worked over the stipulated hours. (Applause) For having charge of this responsible kind of machinery he did nc•t consider that was an exorbitant ware. • (Hear, hear.) In asking for it the men at Messrs. Crosfield's were not making an unreasonable application, especially when they consideied that within the last five years tlJe le men had ii t received a farthing advance. A Voice : It is a lie. Councillor COPLBY : It may be. but lam giving you the information I have received from the men. Con- tinuing, he contended that these men had a right to be censiderel at he preient juncture. In their letter to him the firm referred to the practice of dealing only with their own men, and 6aid they did not see their way clear to deviate from this old standing rule, as it would lead to alterations of the conditions of labour at the works, ' which in the end would only produce a considerable amount of dissatisfaction probably on both sides. Continuing the speaker remarked that the men did not doubt for a moment that the interference of the union might create dissatisfaction on the one side but it would create satisfaction' for the men if they obtained what was reasonable. The firm's letter concluded with the following sentence "Whatever proposals of a practical nature the men feel themselves disposed to lay before the management of the concern will be received with every consideration and if not agreed to it will be because such proposals are found to be absolutely im- practicable from our point of view." He (the speaker) stood before them as a Trade Unionist believing that in that movement and in it only lay his salivation. ' This movement had to be supported by men ho had pluek about them. It wan not necessary for him to stand up for himself, he could protect himself. At the sacrifice of himself he did his best for the brethren who were weaker in argument= speech, and for that he was crucified. But he did not object to being crucified, and as far as his gospel taught him they could expect to receive that treatment. He urged them to be united in that and every town,and it there were employers who acted in an arbitrary manner, their action would soon be a thing of the past. (Applause.) The men were asking for nothing unreasonable. Signing the condition meant that the men would agree that no outsider should be brought in to plead on their behalf, and that the firm should not receive letters frcm outsiders on their behalf, and also that they were satisfied ich the present conditions of work That was all right from the employers' standpoint. He was not present to blame the employers for getting their work done as cheaply as possible if 'they would only deal fairly with the men from the other side. Had the men given notice that meeting would have been somewhat out of court, hut as they received their notice he left the meet- ing to judge whether the men were teine fairly treated or not. Why should rich men like Messrs.Crosfield fight men in their position ? It did uot sbow a gentlemanly feeling. If the firm could prove that they could not afford to pay what was asked the workmen would be reasonable. He believed that the people of Warrington would not be behind in .expressing sympathy for their own citizens. He trusted they were preparei to show their feelings in a respectful and honourable manner.and he was not present to advise anyonete adopt any method that would bring trouble upon him or disgrace on the firm. He trusted that wiser counsels would prevail, and that the gentlemen who he had been told had always expressed sympathy for workmen at Messrs. Crosfiela's ' would bring their influence to bear on the question (Applause.) The resolution was then put to the mesting, and carried with one dissentient. The meeting concluded with a Vote of thanks to the Chairman.

The Firm's Explanation. Mr G Dale writes us as follows on nenalf of Messrs Crosfield'a and Sens. Ltd. About 2-?; years ago, in the summer of IS9B an agree- ment was made between Dr Markel and the firemen with regard to the settlement of all special gr;evanoesi and questions of exceptional importance. The agreement was to the effect that such matters should be brought before the Directors by a deputation appointed by the men. This agreement was made in consequence of a demand for an eight hour; shirt having been received from the Firemen's Union. As a result of this the men's names were omitted from the holiday list for 1898. On applying to Dr Markel to enquire why their names were omitted they were informed "that holidays could not be Riven to men who were evidently dissatisfied with their work." Ultimately a distinct understanding, be- tween themselves and the Managing Director being arrived at on the following poiets their holidays were granted. I—They1—They expressed themselves as setisfiel with the existing conditions of employment. 2—They expressed regret at the course taken by the Union. 3—They promised in the future to brine special griev- ances and questions of exceptional importance directly before the directors. 'lbis understanding continued and the men had their holidays in '9B, '99 and 1900. As soon as the holiday season of 1900 was over an application for an eight hours ebift was received from Mr Copley the General Secretary of the Firemen's Uaion containing the signatures of tee Firemen. The statement made by Mr Copley on Sunday night last to the effect that the men had previously made this application themselves is without foundation. This constituted a direct breach of the agreement. tar Copley was informed that we held to the principle of dealing directly with our men and could not therefore recognise interference from outside parties. After some further correspondenced in which we practically re- peated our original statement, Dr Markel asked for a deputation of the men for the purpose of receiving some explanation direct from the workmen. During the it- t erview one of the deputation said: The Agreement so far as he understood it was only temporary, and not permanent'y binding." Dr Markal replied •• That it wa s binding until a new Agreement was made, and that if they found they could not continue to accept it they should have given due notice to that effect." They were then informed that in order to prevent any misuederstanding in the future the Agreement must be on paper and must be signed by the men This would become an essential condition, and the deputation was asked to inform their fellow work- men that if they wished to remain permanently in our employ they must comply with the condition as stated. After two days the same delegates with one exclAien brought the answer of the men, which was to the effect "that they would not comply with the condition as specifierl, and that they had decided to leave the matter in the hands of the Union." Uron ree, ivinß their decision Dr Markel expressed his regret, but said that under the circumstances he must ask them to inform their fellow workmen that they wet° to consider themselves only temporarily in their employ This was on November Ist. and on November sth the whole of the men concerned received their notice, and accompanying the same a note stating " that if any of the 7meni were prepared to accept the conditions of employment as existing up to date, and would apply to the manager of the department, Mr Dale, they would be re-engaged, providing any vacancies remained." Two of the men astarvraeds expreved their willingness to comply with the conditions of the film. These man were, of course, retained. Two weeks after this the deputetion waited on Dr Markel and expreased their witlineneaa to return to work under the original conciihons They weee ii:- formed that there were no vacancles.—Yours 0 DALE. LADIES' LETTER.

MY . DEAR NELLIE,

There is nothing more becoming than a black velvet hat to a clear complexion. It seems to brighten the pink and cream tints, and to banish the smallest hint of sallowness. White felt, on the contrary, has too often the effect of eliciting and thdn accentuating the yellow that lurks laterv: under every skin. Only the most supremely per- fect complexion can bear the trying proximity. Pink sometimes turns an indifferent complexion almost black. When the shade chosen is very bright and smiling, all the muddy earth-tones come strongly out, and the head-gear, that was chosen to beautify, condemns. The soft tones of beige and almond and forest brown, so fashionable just now, are usually very becoming.

A garmen. which may prove really delightful for evening wear is of the new watered silk gauze, soft and filmy as chiffon, patterned with a light tracery of seed pearls. Made simply—just pouched and drawn into a waistband of gold tissue at the waist, with the neck veiled in folds of deep-toned lace, and a pink rosebud tucked away amongst the lace—this blouse is one, indeed, to covet and acquire. Almost, if not quite, on a par with it are the ones of white chiffon, traced with gold thread, so ethereal and captivating are they. A change of slips underneath these transparent blouses admits of great variety, such as white under black, or vice-versa; coloured slips under black or white, however, go against the grain now, blues and pinks under white muslin being so sug- stestive of a draped toilet cover.

Very popular are floral fetes and balls about Christmas time, and there is this to be said in their favour—they are very easily gut up, and therefore popular in the country where scenic surroundings and dress trappings are hard to come by. A very little ingenuity accomplishes a charming flower dress. Some have trails of flowers, such as rises, violets, or lilies, roped together from the waist downwards, left to:float loosely ovar toile skirts ; others have the skirt strewn with pedals or loose sprays of flowers; whilst on some thick ruchings of flowers border the hems of the flounces.

All skirts continue to be street sweepers, and some absolutely curl upwards in front and at the sides, while they train behind, so they are lengthy out of all reason. Though cashmere and an inter- lining of domette has been tried by a few of the modistes, the really successful firms are still mounting all their skirts upon silk, for whie:i moirette is a very excellent and worthy substitute. Let simplicity be the aim ; buy good materials and trimmings, and not much of the latter, for touches only of passementerie and Lucy braids are requisite, while a big display is not in good taste. Every ancient button, even only a single specinn•ii of its kind, is precious, and so are old cameo waist, clasps and the jewellery.of 45 years ago, inciudii the long and ornate watch.eliain of bright gold every woman of means then possessed. Nock bands, stocks, and ties to be extraordinarily 6111,1 rt, should match the trimming worn, and -wnen Oa) toque, boa and muff are also in keeping the ear,,,t, is very happy. The sack coat in fur is worn by many, bit its success depends as much upon the cut as upon the *cure of the wearer ; and, as a rule it is as smart as it is comfortable. Fur flounces are coming into fashion again, and the owners of rather sliaoby sealskin coats or capes need not hesitate to I).‘-,1 them supplemented with sable, mink, beaver, iox, or undressed seal. The rage of the moment is for silver fox, and as this is a very costly far, as it is one of the rarest, imitations are largely to the fore. Black fox is pointed with whi e hairs to give ic, ~s nearly as possible, the silvery grey blown that characterises the genuine for. Boas with long stole ends are considered veiy smart, though th long ends are rather an inconsequent way of wear- ing fur. Caracul is the fur for smart winter It is as supple and adaptable as velvet, and adds nothing of the clumsy Lind to the liAnye. Your loving_

MESSRS. OROSFIELD AND THEIR WORKMEN.

Alleged:lnterference with the Right to

Combine.

The Co-operative Hall ,Sankey-street, Warrington, was crowded on Sunday evening when a public meeting was held under the auspices of the Amalgamated Union of Enginemen and Firemen, for the purpose of" making known the facts" in relation to a trade dispute at the works of Messrs Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., Bank Quay. In the absence of Mr A C finks schairman of the Trades and Labour Council) Mr F Stott (secretary of the Council) presided, and there were also present on the platform Councillor G H Copley. of Rotherham, general secretary of the Union of Enginemen and Fire- men, and Messrs G Sambre)* (formerly of Warrington, but now of Glasgow), Horrocks,Podmore, Weaver, War- burton, Jones and Hamblen. The CHAIRMAN in opening the proceedings said they were expecting Mr Monks, the president of the Warring- ton Trades and Labour Council to take the chair, but unfortunately be was unable to be present. He was pleased to say that the Warrington Trscles and Labour Council were supporting Messrs Crosfield's men in this dispute. (Hear, hear.) There was a peculiarity about this dispute. It was not a question of hours or of wages but principle, which these men were fighting for—the right to belong to and use the officials of their union for obtaining tbe benefits they were entitled to. So far as he had been able to learn the men affected by this dis- pute worked 72 hours one week and 96 hours the follow. ing week. (Shame.) Messrs Crosfield were applied to by these men for a reduction of hours, some time ago and were refused—Councillor Copley, the general secretary of the Union of Enginemen and Firemen, asking for a reduction of hours for the men belonging to the union who worked there. The outcome of that was that Messrs Croefield wished the men to sign an agreement that they would under no circumstances introduce an official of their society into any dispute they had with tbe firm. Because they would not sign that agreement 30 or 31 of ' the men received their notice of dismissal. There was e footnete to the notice saying that if the men applied individually, and there were any vacancies, they would be re-engaged. It seemed to him (the speaker) that it was not a very nice tiring tor working men to deal cirectly with their employers because those who took notice knew that a man who made himself too promin- ent sas very soma dismissed, and it was likely that these men had they applied directly for an increase of wages and a reduction of hours would have been dl'- missed not because they belonged to a society and asked for better conditions, but a reason wnuld be found. He believed, personally, that those men were quite justified in their position, and did perfectly right in refusing to sign the agreement. (Applause.) Mr. SAMBROOK said he was pleased to be in Warring- ten again—(hear, hear)—but he was sorry to be there on such an occasion and on business of such a regret- table character. Three years ago it was advertised in the papers that the men employed by Messrs. Crosfield. and had been with them for twelve montlas,were entitled to a week's holiday and a week's pay each yeareAt that time seven of the members of this society working there asked for what was being granted to the other men. They got the holiday, and afterwards the firm wished to say that the men made an agreement that no third party i should step in at a time of dispute. The men contra- dicted that statement and have contradicted it all along. 'He (the speaker) went to see Mr. A. H. Crosfield, but he was away. Mr. Henry Roberts told him that the men had broken the aereemeet, and he (the speaker) told him that the men declared there had been no agreement. He (Mr. Sambrook) afterwards saw the men, and wrote to the firm saying that they, with him or without him, wished to meet Dr. Markel and contradict the state- ments made against them. Not having received a reply from the firm he (Mr, Sambrook) brought the question before the Warrington Trades and Labour Council last Tuesday. He did not do so until the reporters had left the room because teey wished to settle the matter without anything appearing in the press, as they bad always endeavoured to do, and as they always tried to do up and down the country. (Hear, hear.) On the fellowing Thursday he received a reply from Messrs. Croefield's stating that another interview could not be granted. He Mr (Sambrook), wired to the head office asking if the General Secretary, could spare the time to come to Warrington. The result was that the matter had to be brought under the notice of the public, es that the bulk of the working men of Warricgton and throughout the country could see whether those present that evening were in the right or wrong If they were in the wrong they would go in, but if they were in the right they would try to gain that right. They would fight for the right as they had always done. (Applause.) They asked those present, if they thought the men were in the right, to help them to gain their rights, and be doing so they would lay the foundation stone for their children to build on after they had gone. (Applause.) A man in the body of the hall rose to ask a question. The CHAIRMAN said a programme had been arranged and thee must adhere to it as much as they could. Mr. HORROCIES submitted the following resolution "That this meeting cf Trades Unionists emphatically protests against the arbitrary and tyrannical action of Messrs Crosfield and Sons, Limited in calling upon some of their workmen to renounce the principle of combina- tion, and hereby declares its determination to insist upon their right to unite for the protection of labour against , the unjust demands of capital." (Applause). In deing so be said he was not like a certain politician wiso said he had no interest to serve directly or indirectly, as he (the speaker) bad a little bit of interest in Trades Unionism. He had no bitter feeling against one firm more than against another. He looked upon all the issues as affecting labour and capital as universal. (Applause). He had that after- noon been readirg Gibbon's Industrial History of England, and it save one a clear idea of what caused trades unionists to come into being. They had had guilds for a considerable number of years, hut trades unionism had its inception 100 years ago. Experience bad taught them that combination was the hest for men, as capital was so well organized. The only thing wortring men lacked was a clear knowledge of the de- mands of capital and the rights of labour. (Hearegrar.) There should be no quarrelling of this description ; they should be en perfect in their organization that these dis- putes could not occur. Mr. Sambrook had stated that certain men at Messrs. Crosfistel's worked 72 helm ens week and 96 hours the following week for £1 7s and £1 15; respectively; and let them remember that this country bad spent nearly £100,000,000 in trying to get votes in South Africa. (Disorder.) They had votes, however, in England and yet such a dispute as the present could exist in their midst. (Bear, hear, and applause.) It was said that an Englishman's home was his castle but what kind of a home was it when the man had to work such long hours for such small wages? The speaker proceeded to show the unrest in the industrial world of t"-day, and said if workingmen were not to lose what they had already gained tbey would have to perfect their organieations and fight unitedly. (Hear, hear.) Be did not blame capitalists for the action they were taking for they werelendeavnur ing to live without work. (Laughter) A workingman had only hie labour to sell and it was his duty to do the best be possibly could for himself. (Applause.) Mr JOSE2II PODMORE junior in seconding the resolu- tion said he had been called upon unexpectedly to per- form that duty. He was sorry that the President of the Trades and Labour Council and other advertised speakers had not been able to torn up. However, as a trades animist, and as a member of the Executive of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, he had the greatest pleasure in seconding the resolution which had been so ably proposed by Mr Horrecke. Some people objected to a meeting being held on a Sunday night, but if religion was anything at all it was good not only for things which concerned them on the Sunday but on the week-day. (Hear, hear.) He thought, therefore, that they were justified sin holding a meeting on a Sunday night because nothing affected the life of a working roan so much as the amount of money he could !et to live upon, with time and recreation to build up character. A man could get on pretty well with an individual employer. He could appeal to his sempathies, and was usually successful, because ro man was so bad as to have no feeling; hut a limited company had neither soul to save nor body to keep. (Laughter.) They did not know with whom they were dealing ; the trembere of a company were surrounded, as it were, by a screen. They could not get to :the individuals who composed the company It seemed to him that the meeting would be perfectly justi- fied in supporting the resolution becauee it was a ques- tion of combination. Men found by experience that by uniting into a collective whole they could get better terms for their labour, shorter hours of work and mere money than by gripe individually to an employer. What did they find where trades unionism did not exist? —low wares, low sanitary enedhiene surrounding the labonr, and long hours, of laheurr Where Trades Union- ism was most powerful they found the best condition of things. (Applause.) The fleet of combination had been fought for by our fore- fathers. They struggled for it threueh persecution, through imprisonment and all kinds of trials and pr2vatiens Thee were row asked to support men who were uphntdir a that principle. (Hear, hear.) These men were asked to sign an agreement that they would go personally to their employees fer the settlement of their Erirv,inres. What die it mean? A Woman's Sad Death.

On Sunday the police received information of the death of Lena Ford, aged 61, who resided at 1, Earle-atreet, and who died in the Infirmary at 2.15 thht afternoon. It appears that at six o'clock on Saturday evening the deceased's son went out of the house, leaving his mother alone. He 'returned shortiy after eleven and found the deceased lying on the floor at. the bottom of: the stairs in an uncon- scious state, bleeding from a wound on the bead. He obtained assistance and deceased was removed to the Infirmary where she died at the time stated without regaining consciousness. It is presumed that Mrs Ford fell downstairs and su-tainerl the injury from which she died. At the inquest on Monday a verdict of " Accidental death" was returned. KITC‘iRR RANGE-3. 'Tile CT rnteq. ST. PAUL'S CHOIR AT THE WORKHOUSE.

The inmatee of the Warrington Workhouse spent an enjoyable hour on Thursday last week its listening to a concert provided in the dining room at the Workhouse by the members of St- Paul's choir, A large number of visitors were present and they had an opportunity of witnessing the bright and cheerful surroundings. unier which the inmates live, despite the fact that they are con fined within the rather sombre pile of buildings known as tbe Workhouse. The ietroduction of the electric light has been a great improvinvint. The dimly- lighted rooms with their flickering gas jets have given place to the cheerful and refulgent light of electricity, and the change is one which amply- justifies the expenditure of the Guardians in this coo nectinn. ((.r7irkhf.) TIIE HOUSE BY THE RIVER.

BY MRS. C. N. WILLIAMSON, Author of "7-e Secret of the Pearls," "The 110U80 by the . ,utk," "Queen Sweetheart," "The Larnstorniers," "Her Royal Highness," tor.

CHAPTER XXVII.

FACE TO FACE,

The restaurant was not yet open, though the employes were going in. Greatly moved and excited iy he marvellous resemblance of the living face to the dead one, and by the coincidence which had brought hiln tom' after the unavailing search of last night, Jack Churchil! waited. After a time that seemed long to him, the shutters were taken down, the front doors opened, as if to allow a fragrance of newly-made coffee to float out into the air.

Jack—the first customer of the day—walked in, his face almost deathly white under its brown coat of sunburn, his eyes dark with excitement. A plain, Jewish-featured girl came forward to serve him ; but he walked past her, further into the restaurant. The superintendent, a middle-aged woman with an ill-tempered, sallow visage, was evidently lecturing a slender, golden-haired girl for some misde- rneanour—a girl whose very back was so like Sybil Wade's that Jack Churchill hardly dared to speak lest a near view • her full face should bring -disillusionment.

"I beg your pardon, • he said, in slightly unsteady tones, addressing the superintendent. "I want breakfast. Is this yoling lady at liberty to bring me some toast and coffee ? " At the sound of his voice the girl turned quickly round, and, as their eyes met, for an instant the room grew dark to Jack. Sybil Wade was not dead; there had been some strange mistake, for this was she, and no other. She knew him, too, and a lovely rose-flush mounted to her forehead. "Mr. Churchill !" the girl stammered, and then looked as if she wished she had kept silence. "Great Heavens! It's really you; you're not dead! Thank God, you're not dead!" The words were uttered in a whisper. Nobody but Sybil could hear them, for the superintendent, with a lifting of her chin, had rustled away to scold someone else. The new customer wanted Miss Ward to serve him, and he could have Miss Ward. She wished him joy of her, for a more absent- minded, careless little wretoh, she had never had about the place. "Did you think I was dead? "asked Sybil, shyly. Then, seeing that all eyes were furtively fastened upon her and the young man, who still wore even- ing dress, though it was eight o'clock in the morn- ing. "Please sit down, and order your breakfast. If you don't, I shall be scolded, for we are not allowed to see friends, unless they come on—on business."

"How can I sit down and let you wait on me?" exclaimed Jack. "But—oh, well, if I must ! Any- thing—anything that's easiest to bring. Eggs— coffee—lots of things, that will keep you here, and give me a chance to talk. For I must talk. If you only knew what I have suffered, and now—to find you alive, as if it had all been some horrible dream I"

Sybil blushed deeply and her eyes widened in surprise. Jack's words had burst forth like a torrent that Wouldn't be stayed; he did not realise all they would reveal to her—but she could not help understanding a little—a very little—of what they really meant. It seemed wonderful that, after all, he had remembered her, that the mistake about her death—the strange, inexplicable inistake--haa caused him pain. To recover her self-possession, she hurried away to give the order for breakfast, and did not return until she *came gravely carrying a tray with steaming coffee, eggs, ,and toast. "Who could have started the story that I was dead ?" she whispered, as she set the dishes on the little marble-topped table. "It must have been some alp r girl with the same name, for evidently Lady Carstairs believed it, as —as my cousin, Gladys—" She stopped, remembering what had been post- poned until after the funeral." "I don't see how that is possible." said Jac*, gravely, "for, of course, they had both met you, so many times since that first night." Sybil shook her head. "Never since then."

Jack stared, bewildered. "But Lady Carstairs called on you the next day, after—after your father's death. And then she and Gladys have been so often to the nursing home-" "What nuriing home ? "broke in Sybil, blankly.

"Why, where -you were staying in the country- ; they never told me where, exactly, but they occasionally mentioned when I saw them in the evening having been out to you in the morning or afternoon, so I knew the place could not be far from town. And then-"

"But I've not been in a nursing home," stam- mered Sybil, utterly dumbfounded. "Lady Carstairs never came to. see me anywhere." _

Jack Churchill gazed t her, and could not speak. His very lips were white. "What does it all mean ? " he muttered,_ half under his breath.

"I don't know," echoed Sybil. "It is, indeed, like a dream. But all my life has been mystery and fear, fear and mystery, since the night I met you—the night my father died." "Will you trust me? Will you tell me ? " Jack implored, in a low, eager voice. "Not here—we can't speak freely; but, for Heaven's sake, don't stop in this place another moment. It's like sacrilege almost for you to be here ! Say to that uoman that you are going awly—now, instantly, and—"

"I can't do that," Sybil interrupted. "I'm only here till a girl whose people have been very, very kind to me can come back to work. She has been ill, but she's almost well now, and it is arranged that she returns this afternoon. If I should go, it would make the superintendent very angry, and perhaps—almost surely—she would visit my fault on poor Fanny Atkins. I must stay till afternoon, for her sake ; and besides "

But again she checked herself, and her cheeks were stained with carnation.

"Besides what? I beg that you'll finish, to shew, at least, that you are willing to trust me as your friend." "Ah, I do trust you—though things have hap- pened lately that have made me feel I could never again trust anyone on earth. But, somehow, you are different. I know I can trust you." "I'd gladly give my life to serve you," said Jack, forgetting Gladys, and letting his heart look out through his eyes. "Tell me what you were going say?"to. __ . .

"dilly that—l don't quite know what is going to become of me when I leave this place. But, of course, I shall manage somehow." "Your friends will manage for you." "I have no friends. I had one, who saved my life when I was in terrible danger a few weeks ago, but she has disappeared; and I think I am the loneliest girl in the whole world." "I must know something that has happened to you since that dinner at Lady Northmuir's," pleaded Jack. "There's a mystery that, for every- one's sake, must be cleared away; the sooner the better. I beg that you will grant me what I ask, for I want to be your friend, I want to be the one to help you—more than I ever wanted anything else in my life." Hester had earnestly warned Sybil to trust no one, and the girl's own experiences of late had certainly not encouraged her to thoughtless con- fidences ; but Jack Churchill, as she had said, seemed different from anyocie else in the world, and instinct told her that he was true as steel. She tried to realise that his interest in her must be entirely because of her cousinship with Gladys, who was to be his wife, but she could not always remember, as she hurriedly told her story, and watched the changing expression of his face; for sometimes it appeared as if he thought of her alone, not at all of Gladys. She made short work of the tale, relating it simply, dwelling more on the part which had con- cerned, first her father, then Hester, than that which had to do with herself. Jack listened with- out interruption to the end, but his eyes were blazing with a light which she could not understand. "It must seem to you almost as if I were mad," she said at last, rather timidly, "for the story is so strange, so absolutely incomprehensible. Yet I can't explain anything, for I don't see what is behind the veil of mystery any more than you do"

"But I think that I ain beginning to see," Jack answered.

"You really believe—in spite of its appearing so reasonless—that there has been a plot against us— Hester and me ? "

"I do believe it; a plot against your father and you, and this poor, brave Hester, because of what she did for you, because of what she knew and might tell if she chose. There are some things in the world, even in these days at the end of the nineteenth century, which are too strange not to be irtlA t and this is one of them." said jack. "Great

Heavens! What a plot! lam only just beginning to understand. But, by-and-bye, I shall see it all Clearly. Meanwhile, I want you to leave this piaci, now with me, and let me Lake you to friewt,. who will be true to you."

t now, please not now," pleaded 6f)il. "I must stay till Fanny Atkins comes bacl,—only till this afternoon. It would be ungrateful in me to selfishly do something which would set the super- intendent against her." "The woman can have any amount of money sha wants to let you go," said Jack. "Oh, don't look at me like that! lam your friend, and friends can do anything for one another. Besides, if lam not very much mistaken, there's a good deal of money that ought somehow to be yours—exactly how, I don't know—and some clay it /till be yours. 6`l-kp you see, .yon can pay back" any debts—if you il them debts—with interest.' .

'II wish it might be so ! " 'sighed Sybil. "I'd .ae to a, so much for liester-when she is found, and for the Atkinses, too. I couldn' !, offer to bribe the superintendent. It would only make things worse. And—and nob(rly here likes me very much; they would talk and say unkiad things. I must wait."

"When will your friend come?" asked Sack. "Perhaps about five or six o'clock, so that she will only have two or three hours' work before she can go home to rest twain when we shut up. To- morrow she expects to be strong enough, though, to go on as usual. "And now you must go, for 3ou have been very good, -pretending to eat your breakfast, and if you stay longer the superintendent and the gills will be even more curious than they are now." Jack rose reluctattly. He could hardly bear to leave her now that Heaven had so mercifully brought them together. But he had not the right to insist upon his will. -"I am afraid to go," he said, bluntly. "Your enemies have found you out before; they may again." "Oh, not here, lam sure she exclaimed. "I am lost; I have left no traces behind. Only. think I have been here more than a week, and perhaps they—whoever they are—may believe me dead, as you did." "More likely that they wish others to believe it," murmured Jack. But, as she said, it did not seem probable that "they ", (whose names he began to fear he could supply) had for the present lost track of her. In a few hours, if all went well, she would be safe from villainy, and the Damocles sword of punishment would be hanging over the heads of those who had injured her. "Good-bye, then, if you will have it be," he said, "or rather, au revoir." "Au revoir," repeated Sybil, smiling. He held out his hand and she shyly laid hers in his, to feel it'grasped as if he could never let it go. But .he did let it go at last. And in a few moments Sybil was gazing wistfully through the window at Jack Churchill's tall figure as once he looked back, and it seemed that their eyes met. "In a few more hours," he reassured himself again, little guessing what those few hours were to bring forth.-

CHAPTER XXVIII.

MSS MONSTANCE MARES A CALL.

Miss Monstance had not come to the ."Busy Bee" restaurant without mentally arranging a definite plan of action. She knew exactly what she •;-)tnted to do (thanks in part to a conversation lately held with Lord Carstairs), and from the moment of entering the door she set briskly about doing it. _ . _

There was one thing of which she wished to make sure before proceeding further, as she would look exceedingly foolish in the eyes of her employer if she brought him the wrong person. A glance round the room shewed her that she had come to the right place to find Sara Ward. But Carstairs had questioned Miss Monstance about the wearing apparel of the girl he wanted to find, and, showing her a torn, bit of serge, had asked if the Miss Ward who worked in the Addressing Agency had ever appeared in a frock of that material and that colour. Miss Monstance had assured him that Sara Ward never wore anything else, and then, with a look of satisfaction, he had given her the piece of stuff asking ber• to try and ascertain whether there was, or had been, a tear in the frock which matched the bit in shape. "Well, this is a surprise, Miss Ward!" exclaimed the young woman from the Addressing Agency, making her way to Sybil who had just finished serving an old lady. "Who would ever have thought of seeing you here? So you've left the agency as well 'as me ? " "Didn't you know I had left ? " said Sybil, flushed with the unpleasant surprise of beholding her old enemy. ' "Not I. My mother was taken ill. I gave in notice one Saturday and never turned up at the agency again. She's all right-now. But I'm not going back'for I've got a place as private secretary to a gentleman, and only work five days of the week. I've been shopping in the Fulham-road this morning, and I'm so hungry. Do bring me a cup of tea and a fried eep., there's a good girl, and don't look as if you tliought.l was going to eat you up. I was cross at the agency, I admit that now I'm out of it, but I was afraid you were after my place in the end, you were on such good terms with Mr. Robertson. I daresay I wasn't as nice as I might have been to you and Miss Carter, but I'm sorry now—l am, honest true. I hope you'll forgive and forget, as the saying is." She spoke so cordially that Sybil was disarmed, and felt guilty because her heart did not warm easily towards Miss Mons;ance. "If there was ever anything to forgive I've already forgotten it," she responded. "I won't be long with your breakfast." "There's a funeral to-day out at the house where my new boss lives," Miss Monstance slangily announced when Sybil returned with a tray; so I'm off on my own, though this is supposed to be one of may working afternoons. However, I shouldn't wonder if I went out there, anyhow, for I 'shall see some rather interesting people if I do. Mr. Smith's the name of my employer. He's an author and a friend of lots of swell people. Lord Carstairs is his most intimate, who's put one of his country places, called Carstairs House, at Mr. smith's service while he's writing a book. There's dear old housekeeper and lots of servants to look after him though the family haven't lived there for years, and the place agrees with him though it's said to be damp. Lately there's been an invalid there, though he saw nothing of her, hardly knew she was in. the house till she up and died a few day,s ago. This afternoon she'll be buried-in the old family vault, as. she's a poor relation of Lord Carstairs. And Lady Carstairs, the young lord's mother, with his sister, Miss Gladys Wade, are going out to the funeral. I'd like to see .Miss Wade, as she's supposed to be one of the great beauties of the day; going to marry a millionaire, too. Mr. Smith said I might came if I liked, He'd put me in a room where I could peep out and have a glimpse of what went on without any- one's seeing me. -Rather interesting, oh?" "Yes," replied Sybil, thotightfully. "Look here," exclaimed Miss Monstance, apparently speaking on impulse. "To show you that it's all right between us now I'll tell you what I'll do. If you can get away from here I'll take you with me if you like." "Thank you," said Sybil.. name of the girl who died?" "I think she was a Miss Wade, too."

"Do you know the

"Shall you see her face in the coffin before she's taken away to the church ? " "I can if I go out early enough before the grand neople come. Want to go ? " "I should if I could get away from here in time, but I know I can't do that. The girl whose place I'm taking, Fanny Atkins, thought this morning she could come in by five or six. But, of course, that would be too late."

"I'm afraid it would," returned Miss Monstance.!" "Too bad! I'd rather have you with me than go alone. 'When I finish my shopping I'll just pop in again, to see if by any chance your friend's come and you can get off." Sybil thanked her and the conversation came to an end, Miss Monstanee appearing deeply absorbed, in her tea and eggs. She Was thinking very hard, for though she had earned a hundred pounds, a second hundred depended upon getting the girl out to Carstairs House. As Sybil talked Miss Monstance-had stealthily produced the torn rag of serge, and, fingering cautiously. the folds of her ! companion's dress, as Sybil stood close to the chair' in which she was seated, she had found a neatly mended break. In texture and shape the bit of serge in her possession was most satisfactory as a match, and Miss Monstance was reassured. There ! was now no fear that Mr. Smith could wriggle out 0 his bargain by saying she had fixed upon the wrong person. EU had exactly obeyed all.his instructions, ginu

in case she should rind miss ward, and was pleased with the way in which she had done it. Her employer had not taken her into his confidence,. but ske "X&S shrewdly certain that his real name was much more distinguished than Smith, even if he were not Lord Carstairs himself. She was sure, too, that he had some more potent reason fox wishing to discover the whereabouts of Miss Ward than the stolen MSS. he had mentioned, but what- ever the reason might be it was not love. Some day, perhaps, she might learn, or at least guess the truth. But should there be anything "queer in the story" (as she expressed it in thought), she would never be safe in telling what she knew even for a reward. It would be wiser to hold her tongue, for she was playing catspaw, and rather a dark case might be made out against her. Her best plan would be to "lie low" and get what she could from her own principal. Now it would not do. to let the scheme fall through because Sara Ward could not leave her work until evening. The funeral, of which Miss Monstance had talked with "Mr. Smith," was the only excuse by which she could hope to lure the younger girl to this Carstairs House, therefore they must go in the afternoon. She must not let her- self appear too eager, however, or Sara Ward might suspect that her motive was something more than good nature. _ _ _ _

A little more reflection turned her mind in the direction of Fanny Atkins. She had already heard the name, and knew that the Atkins family lived in Rosemary-mews. Saying nothing to Sybil of her intention she paid her bill, bade/ Miss Ward good-bye till afternoon, much as Jack Churchill had done not so long before, and departed.

She went at once lo ktoseintu-y-me-a s, ascended to the upper floor occupied by the Atkitses, and, being admitted by a stout, middle-aged dame, announced herself as an old friend of Miss Ward, who had just been talking with her at the "Busy Bee." There was a great chance of a splendid situation as companion for Miss Ward if only she could get away early that afternoon and be presented to the lady before other applicants arrived. She (Miss Monstance) hadn't mentioned this to Miss Ward, lest in the end she should' be disappointed, merely asking if she could go out in the afternoon to a place called Carstairs House. Miss Ward had refused, as she could not get away in time; but one of the young ladies in the restaurant had spoken of Miss Fanny Atkins and Rosemary-Mews, so Miss Monstance had made bold to come and see if there was any hope that Miss Atkin's could run round to the restaurant a little earlier than she was expected and relieve Miss Ward, Kind Mrs. Atkins spoke to Fanny, and Fanny, who was really a good deal better, thought she might manage it. Miss Monstance expressed her- aelf delighted to hear this, but only begged that Miss Atkins wouldn't mention her having come round to make the suggestion. If poor little Miss Ward dreamt that Miss Atkins had not turned up at the restaurant early of her own accord she would never believe her kind friend was really well enough to work, and she would probably decline to go. Then she would lose the most splendid opportunity that a girl had ever had. The Atkinses, mother and ' daughter, admitted the justice of this remark, and it was decided that, immediately after the noonday dinner,' Fanny should present herself at the "Busy Bee" quite as if the impulse had come without prompting from outside. It was already nearly twelve, and by one o'clock Fanny promised to be at the restaurant. Primed with this good news Miss Monstance departed, occupying her time as best she could in two or three shops until nearly one. Then she returned to the ".Busy Bee," purposely appearing a few minutes earlier than Fanny Atkins was expected to arrive that no suspicion of collusion might arise. As she anticipated Sybil informed her that Fanny had not come; and having lingered a little on one pretext or another, pretended to be in the act of going when who should bustle in but Fanny Atkins I 'Now you can go with me, can't you ? " cried Miss Monstance, when the two girls had spoken together and ,Fanny had explained according to agreement. "If I do," said Sybil, "I must be here again by five o'clock, as about that time I—l expect a friend to call. I don't know where Carstairs House is, but you say it's in the country. Can Igo with you now and be back before five—surely?" "Surely," echoed Miss Monstance. "It isn't very far.' She had been particularly instructed to say, nothing about the river, and though Carstairs House was close to the river there was a way in, across a lawn from the high road, which gave but few glimpses of th,. river through closely growing trees. "Miss 'Ward" had been there before, Miss Monstance bad been told, and must not cuspect that she was being taken to the same place again; therefore she must go by an entirely iff rent, far leas circuitous rode, and Miss Monsiance knew precisely what this route was to be. Indeed, lest her memory should fail at a crisis, she had it all written down on paper. They drove to Waterloo Station, tork a train pat ready to leave for Kingston, and Otero Miss lionstance had been told to take a carria2e.

treat eincident lightly on their arrival at - Padre Maynard's house, yet the old clergy- man's kindly face gave token of his pertur- bation before the story had been half told. "I'm sorry now," said he, "that I was not more insistent upon Hilda's departure •to Cape Town when the danger first threatened. . it, I considered it my duty to stay here, to help and counsel my people in the dark hours that have come upon us, still there was no such necessity on Hilda's part. Yes, I regret now that I ever yielded to her entreaties to remain by my Side through the-"

"Please don't go over all that again, father," interrupted his daughter, laugh- ingly. "Why, you are not yet even quite recovered from your recent illness. How could I have had the heart to leave you all alone? It would have been wicked, inhuman of me. And what a dreadful Christmas I' should have spent, safe myself, yet con- stantly worried with fears and anxiety about you, father. I could not have borne it! It would have been worse—a thousand times worse—than anything I can possibly suffer here, so that I am near you. Besides, even if you had persisted I should have refused to go. So long as you stay in Walenstad, so Long will I stay, too. There, now ! " "-I'm afraid you have no choice to-day, dear," added her father, smiling at her vehemence. "It would be all but impossible for anyone to quit the town without the express sanction of the Free State leaders." "How's that?" Gerald put in, startledly. "The place is not yet in Boer hands, any- way." "No, but it is hemmed in on every side," Mr. Maynard replied. "Owing to its exposed situation, Walenstad is incapable of effective resistance even if there were any forces or armaments available for its protection. It may be occupied by the invaders at any time now. In fact, I gather that Command- ant Klein is marching this way and will probably enter the town on Monday at the latest. Meanwhile the Depeview Command() has swept round to 'the west, cut the tele- graph, and thrust in a wedge of burghers between us and Naauwpoort. So to speak, we are almost as completely isolated as is Kimberley itself—only, of course, there is no 'need for a siege here." "By Jove! that's unpleasant news to *ar, ' cried Gerald, uneasily. "And it's not all the tidings I have to tell you, either," the padre went on, quickly; nor, perhaps, the most vital." By this time, night rapidly closing in, the lamp had been lit 4nd placed on the table. The window itself, near which Mr. Maynard and his visitor were sitting, was not shut although the outer jalousies had been drawn together, thus allowing the cool air to pass freely into the room. Hilda, - leaving the two men to their chat, hurried off to the kitchen to superintend the preparations for supper. "It was this further news that I particu- larly wanted to discuss with you, Gerald," said Mr. Maynard after a slight pause. "Soon after midday a Basuto runner brought me a. couple of letters from Mr. Wilbert, the resi- dent magistrate at Maysheim—one addressed to myself, and the other to the General Officer in Command, at Naauwpoort Camp.' " "But Maysheim has been held by the Boers for some time past, and Mr. Wilbert is virtually a prisoner there!" "Precisely. They keep a strict watch over him, and, have forbidden him to leave the town; yet his personal freedom is only restricted with those limits. And that free- dom he has used to considerable profit in ascertaining the numbers, equipment, and strategicalintentions of the Republicans. The result of his secret investigation he well knew would be of incalculable value if he coukl only get them through to the British general. Ultimately one of the natives undertook to deliver the document to ME here • and this hazardous task, by great good luck and after many narrow squeaks, he has successfully carried out. So far, so good." Mr. Maynard opened an escritoire and drew forth a thin roll of papers. "Here we are ! " said he. "And now the question is—how am I to forward them past the cordon of Boers to Naauwpoort? 01 course Mr. Wilbert did not forsee that might be in much the same sort of fix as he himself. There are few men hereabout who could be trusted with such a mission, and fewer still who would care to attempt it, seeing that failure and capture would in- evitably mean instant death as a spy. Time presses, too. The enterprise will become more impracticable as soon as the enemy occupy Walenstad itself."

Geld stopped thrumming upon the chair with his fingers, and flashed a keen look into the speaker's expectant face. "I quite follow you," said he, calmly and resolutely. "Yes, ni make the attempt. Now—to-night!" Leaning forward, the padre clasped the young fellow's hand warmly. "I knew I could rely on you, my boy," he murmured, with feeling. "Had I been in a fit condition to undergo a thirty-mile ride myself, you would not have had the option. But if it can be done at all, you are the very man to pull it through. You are fs,miliar with every inch of the country, have a head on your shoulders, and a physique tough as whipcord. By carefully mapping out the route to be traversed, I firmly believe the thing is feasible."

Orthe various suggestions made during the next quarter of an hour it is unnecessary •to speak in detail here; suffice it to learn that eventually Gerald decided to direct his course to the northern spurs of the Zuurberg Mountains, across the open Karoo and well to the east of the Boer laager —avoiding the bridge over the Oorlogs Spruit, which was sure to be jealously guarded—and thence feeling his way cautiously round the enemy's outposts. "By keeping to the track through the Zwart Kloof, just beyond Vanderst's Farm." Gerald concluded, "I shall be pretty well screened from the the sharpest of eyes for a good part of the ride, especially if the night turns out fairly black- Once on the far side of the Oorlogs—which is fordable at most points in this dry season—the rest of the journey ought to be plain sailing." "Very true," was his companion's com- ment. "Then you'd better put these dis- patches in your pocket at once. Mr. Wilbert tells me he has not appended his name to them lest they should fall into the wrong bands; but you will be able to guarantee their genuineness if any question should be —Hark! What on earth was that?" A faint grating noise, as of a stealthy footfall on the stoep (verandah) outside the window, was quickly followed by a dull thud on the gravel beneath. "By thunder ! " ejaculatt Gerald. "An eavesdropper ! " He darted to the casement, flinging wide the shutters and craning his neck out over the sill. This way and that he peered through the darkness, seeing no movement but the swaying shadows of the trees and shrubs cast by the new-risen moon, hearing nothing save the gentle rustle of their leaves. Withdrawing his head, he spun round to find Hilda standing ashca-lipped in the doorway. " Oh, father !" she gasp- !. disjointedly. "Someone—just now—ran p:;.st the kitchen window! He jumped over the fence at the end of the garden.' "You recognised him, Hilda?" " I—l thought so, father. His repulsive race has seemed to haunt me all this day. But, perhaps I was mistaken. After all, I could not be sure, it was too dark to see distinctly; and I merely caught a glimpse of hint as he rushed past. The suddenness of it startled and frightened me." "Poor child !" responded the minister, caressingly. "You are overwrought and fanciful to-night, I fear. Better get off to bed directly after supper; a sound sleep will rob you of all such foolish imaginings. As to the prowler, whoever he may be, we'll trouble our heads no more about him. I'll be bound it was some unconscionable rogue after my water-melons again!"

X A

A dozen miles or so from Walenstad lies the Zwart Kloof, a deep—scarped defile in the Zuurberg foot-hills, boulder-strewn and interspersed with clumps of vaal brush, its southern end opening on .the banks of the Oorlogs River. A radiant moon threw the eastern slope into black shadow, its serrated crest being thus projected in clear-cut out- line against the base of the opposite bluff. Drear, sombre and desolate, a spot subtly in keeping with the ruthless crime of which it was to become the scene this Christmas morn.

Midway down its length, immediately above the tortuous path, crouched Jakob Fesche—a rifle resting on his knees, his eyes and ears sharply alert. An hour or more he must have lurked there, when of a sudden he tightened his clutch on the gun-barrel and poked its grim muzzle round an angle of his shelter. A series of metallic clicks, the ringing of iron shoes on stone, grew louder and nearer minute by minute, until out of the darkness there bulked the vague shape of a horse and its rider, picking their way along the valley at a slow foot pace. At a point not more than twenty yards from where Fesche lay, the horseman's figure, sharply silhouetted against the moonlit background, offered a conspicuous mark. And Fesohe ifulled the trigger. With the report of the weapon, the horse stumbled to its knees, recovering itself to rear and plunge violently before finally wheeling round and tearing back frantically towards the head of the kloof. But it went riderless! On the ground Gerald Venn lay motionless, his pallid face upturned to the stars and a thin black stream trickling down it from the wound in his forehead.

"That settles your score, at any rate, you cursed rooinek snarled Fesche, stooping over the lifeless body. "And now I'm free to scrape what profit I can out of it. First, the letters I "

He plunged his hand inside Gerald's coat and transferred the all-important package of papers to his own pocket, supplementing it with whatever cash and valuables be could put his avaricious fingers on. Then, clamber- ing down the ravine to where his horse had been hobbled, he loosed it and vaulted into the saddle. Soon he was galloping over the veldt in the direction of the Boer camp at Oorlogs Pont, nine miles away. Fully half the distance had been covered— Fesche inflating his pride with thoughts of the praise, flatteries and favours with which his devotion to the cause would be recog- nised by the Boer Commandant—when he was vividly struck by a fresh idea, whereby he might secure a reward of a more satis- factory and tangible kind. Why should he be fobbed off with mere empty thanks when; by carrying the papers straight on to Naauwpoort, he could get gold—much gold— for them?

"The English General will pay any price I ask," he ruminated, craftily. "What's to stop me? If one man hoped to slip through safely, so can another. Ach, it's worth the risk—l'll do it!"

Scarcely had he pulled his horse rlund, however, before a peremptory challenge in guttural Dutch sang out on the night air. Stricken with acute fear, his alarm augmented perhaps by reason of the doubly traitorous game he had in contemplation, Fesche bent down close to his nag's body, rammed his heels into its flanks, and bolted in headlong panic for his life. Behind him he heard the rapid patter of mounted men in pursuit—a sound ere long succeeded by the harsh crackle of Mauser rifles and the vicious "phut-phut I" of their bullets.

===== 0002601_19001221_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER IV.

Meanwhile there had been other happen. ings in Zwart Kloof. Jakob Fesche was not the unerring marks- man he plumed himself on being, inasmuch as his shot, instead of making a bole in Gerald Venn's skull, had really only taken a snippet off the rim of his horse's ear. In its subsequent stumble and wild bucking:4 the frenzied brute had unseated its rider, whose head had struck against one of the ironstone rocks as he crashed heavily to earth. Stunned and inert, he lay there like one already dead. But he was worth any number of eoffined men yet. Within a few minutes of Fesche's disappearance from the Kloof, Gerald re- gained consciousness and struggled up on his elbow. Still numb and half-stupel, he had not yet accounted far his present sorry predicament before a clatter of hoofs caused him to look round in perplexity. Two horses were crossing the white belt of moonlight below, the bridle of one held by the woman who rode the second animal.

Even at that distance, despite his aching eyes, Gerald recognised all three—his own chestnut, Hilda's pony, and—Hilda herself In a little while the girl was kneeling beside him, full of pity, care and womanly tenderness.

"An awkward spill I've had, nothing more," said Gerald in reply to her anxious inquiries. "If it wasn't for the buzzing in my head I should be as right as—But you, Hilda—you here! What does it mean ?" With the intuition of a born nurse, Hilda put aside his questions until she had assured herself of the slightness of his injuries; then, as she staunched the blood and bandaged his gashed forehead with her handkerchief, she made answer:

"This is all Jakob Fesche's evil doing! I know—l feel certain of that, Gerald." Her words brought Gerald's memory back to him, and with it a whirl of harassed thought. He staggered erect and clapped his hand to his breast pocket. "The papers—gone ! ' he groaned, aghast. "I remember now. That scoundrel must have stolen them from me. It was all a murderous trap to kill and rob me of them." "I feared as much—l dreaded it," said Hilda shuddering. "When father, soon after you had started, told me of the under- taking you were engaged on, the recollection of that man's skulking near the house came back at once to my mind. What if he had really been listening to your plans and arrangements all the time? I knew he would stop at nothing to do you harm, Gerald. I dared not mention my suspicions to father, for he could have done nothing to :help or warn you. Yet my fears were not to be set aside. I knew it would be impossible for me either to sleep or rest until—and—and so I followed you myself!" "But your fatherr" "die does not know—he would not have let me go. After bidding him good-night I slipped out of the house instead of going up to my room, saddled the pony and rode my hardest—hoping to overtake you and urge you to follow a different route from the one you had fixed op. I thought Fesche would probably gallop ahead to betray your plans to the Boers, so that they might waylay and cut you off. I fancied that that was what had actually happened when, near Vanderst's Farm, I saw your horse trotting back home without you. He recognised my voice and came whinnying to me when I called." "By Jove, Hilda!" cried Gerald, in un- feigned admiration. "For downright nerve and grit you're a girl in a million! It's a pity such pluck should be wasting in lae,dling a blind ass like myself out of the hole I've blundered into." _ _ _ _

"Don't, Gerald—don't 1" protested she, quaveringly. "Besides, you are not out of danger yet. At any moment Fesche may bring the Boers uponus ." "1 warrant they'll be in no vast hurry to pay their respects to a corpse. Why should they? And the nearest of them must be miles away, or they'd have swooped down like vultures on bearing Fesche's shot. But still, you're right, Hilda. The sooner we clear out of this gruesome den, the better. Let me help you into the saddle—so! Now we can ride back together." "Oh, no—no !" interposed Hilda quickly. "I am going back, as I came—alone. I insist, Gerald. No, I won't listen! Aftel what has occurred, your life would be even less safe in Watenstad than if you took your chances and pushed on for Naa.uwpoors to-night."

iNEI;, FRIDAY, DECEMBER

Gerard saw her resolution was -Wei 'CO TM shaken. Beneath the seeming wilfulness of an impulsive girl, he fancied at last he could divine the real emotions which had moved her to such daring and self-denial. And his heart grew buoyant within him. "Believe me, Hilda," said he, gravely; "but for one thing I should have left Walen- sLad weeks ago. It was solely the thought of being near you if troubles befell, the readier to guard you from every—" "Yes, Gerald—l think I understand," murmured she, softly. "Yet you were wrong to hesitate. There was far greater need for you elsewhere—a higher call upon your strength, your courage and your loyalty. And now it is I who bid you fulfil that claim—if for none other, then for my

sake." "Hilda, three minutes ago I thought this day was likely to be the blackest the world could have in store for me. But now I dare to hope it will turn out the luckiest Christ- mas that ever dawned—" " Perhaps—l trust—for both of us," added his companion, in a whisper. "No, do not delay a moment longer. Go, go now, Gerald! And God grant this bitter strife may soon be at an end." Gerald pressed her hand to his lips in farewell, and stood aside with bowed head until the darkness had hidden her from view. Then, swinging into the saddle, he turned his horse's head southward again. Owing to his intimate knowledge of the Locality, coupled with the extreme caution and vigilance he exercised on the journey, Gerald did not fall in with a solitary soul before he ultimately drew rein at the impera- tive " Halt ! who comes there?" of the British picket outside Naauwpoort. Subsequently he was enrolled as a trooper in Major Rimington's Scouts—a strenuous band whose usefulness, dash and gallantry during the campaign are now matters of history. Any anxiety he may have felt as to the welfare of Hilda and her father was set at rest on New Year's Day by their appearance in the British camp with other refugees who had been accorded safe-conduct from Walen- stad on the occupation of that dorp by Commandant Shortly afterwards, in spite of the demands of his new duties, Gerald managed to find an opportunity for explaining to her father how affairs, stood between him and Hilda. Mr. Maynard, smiling, held out his hand. "I'm glad it is so," said be, earnestly-. "There's no man breathing to whom I'd rather entrust my little girl's happiness. After the war—and Heaven send that you come safe out of it—we must lose no time in setting the wedding-bells achime. I warrant you'll fight none the worse for having some- thing personal to win at the end of it, eh, Gerald? Fight, say I! Why, when I saw those Transvaalers marching into Walenstad the other day, I felt almost like shouldering a rifle myself. However, all things con- sidered, I suppose I ought to deem myself fortunate in getting clear of the town with a whole skin.' Indeed, the padre might reasonably have looked for more embarrassing attentions from the Boers had they ever suspected his share in the attempted transmission of Mr. Wilbert's letters. Why they did not is sufficiently clear from the subjoined cable- gram, extracted from the London news- papers of a week's later date. As will be seen, the communique came originally from the Transvaal capital—then, of course, still the headquarters of ex-President Kruger :- ‘‘ PRETORIA. (via Lourenco Marques). "A dispatch from Colesberg states that early on Christmas morning a man was shot dead by a patrol party of the Depeview commando while he was trying to pass through the Federal lines near Oorlogs Pont. He has since been identified as a resident of Walenstad, one whose sympathy in the cause of the Republics has long been under suspicion. From important papers found upon his body there can be no doubt he had been acting as a spy in the British interests."

21, 1900

NEW BOOKS, MAGAZINES,

ETC.

Mr. F. Marion Crawford's new story," In the Palace of the King," is one of his best; taking con- centrated interest and rapid action as the main characteristics of a novel it is perhaps his best, for surely there was never more incident crowded on the pages of romaimsM lifette of, happenings compressed in SO 'Short a perkier. It is one of Mr Crawford's peculiarities to write a story brimful of everything the reader wishes, and yet cover no loager a period than a few days or boars. "In the Palace of tale King,' is a striking example, for the whole novel begins, has its being and ends in the course of one night. If you consider this fact when reading the story then it is a drawback,for the mind refuses to believe in the possibility of such a whirl- wind of passion and pathos being experienced in the time, and there is cons-3quently a constant mental effort to credit it, to the detriment of the author's word. But pick the novel up with the sole idea of obtaining enjoyment, settle down com- fortably to peruse and you will soon be head over heels in the fortunes of Don John and Dolores di Mendoza, whose world is the Court of the Spanish King Pnilip 11. The title of the story is correct even in detail for the royal habitation of King Philip is never left for an instant. Fortunately it is vast enough to accommodate the plot‘and incident of Mr. Crawford's novel. It is purely a love story,the old, old story of love battling against odds and winning in the end, but there is no mawkish sentimentality about the lovers. Their passion is the passion of the Middle Ages described with a simpleness and force which create a deep impression on the mind of the reader. The characters on the stage are ex- cellent, the picture of the crafty unscrupulous king being drawn with a master-hand. There is not a dull page "in the Palace of the King ' and the reader who can put it down before coming to a reluctant step at " the end" must indeed be cold- blooded. There are some very suitable illustrations from the pen of Fred Roe, and the book is turned out in the style one expects from Messrs. Mac- millan's six-shilling series, Miss F M Peard's new story "Number One and Number Two" deals with the complicated love affairs of a charming but wilful heroine. Her first lover has proved faithless, and she is taking her [revenge on other adorers when she comes to Egypt and finds Number One has fallen under a cloud and enlisted under a feigned name. Number rwo is sent by her in search of him and nurses him through a dangerous illness in a remote Nile village. Unfortunately Number Two does not realise that the heroine now has only resentment for the man she first loved, and it is only after many misunderstandings that his devotion is re- warded.—There are charming descriptions of Egypt and of Gibraltar, where the story ends happily, for everypne. It is well-written and pleasing. (Macmillan and Company, 65.) Messrs. Macmillan and Co., have commenced a uniform edition of Mr F Marion Crawford's novels at 3s 6d, the first from the press being" Mr leaacs," the brilliant story of Indian life which won Mr Crawford his initial success. There are few countries which Mr. Crawford has not written of since then, and Ile seems equally at home in them all. His series of Italian novels is one of the finest contribu- tions to present day fiction from any author, while America, Germany, Sicily, Russia, Arabia, and this country all owe Mr. Crawford something for the studies he has made of them and their peoples. In short the series will be an encyclopmdia of miscel- laneous and interesting humanity in all times and places, and in addition a collection OT perhaps, on the whole, the most readable novels that have been published in our generation. Rhoda Broughton is usually styled a girls' writer with something more complimentary than con- temptuous in the description. We suppose it is necessary for girls to read as well as other people, and they are exceedingly well catered for by Miss Rhoda Broughton,who provides them both quantity and quality. Her latest production is " Foes in Law ' (Macmillan and Co.' 6s) and very entertain- ing reading it is. Lettice Trent, the heroine, is a conventional but essentially human maid with an elder brother as guardian. The latter so far forgets his guardianship as to marry the most flighty and flippant of beauties, with the result that in addition to becoming sisters-in-law, the two girls became "foes in law." There is nothing serious happens— Miss Broughton can dispense admirably with tragedy,—but the story does not lack incident or fall short of being graphic in every detail. Our lady readers will find much in it that appeals to their , interest and emotion, -

It is not often that we find millionaires entering in all seriousness the field of fiction, but there is at least one of them with courage enough to do so. , and that one is Mr. William Waldorf Astor. Unkind people might say, perhaps, that not much courage is required when millions are behind, but that is not the point at all. Mr. Astor has written some- thing for us to read, and he must not mind it he is criticised by his readers. To begin with, much of the merit of "Pharaoh's Daughter," (Macmillan and Co., 65.) and other stories, seems to be in its out- ward appearance. There is gilt on the cover, gilt on the edges of the pages, and exceedingly taste- ful illustrations on the best art paper inside. The book is an undoubted triumph of letterpress, and there are several of the stories which are not with- out merit. Their author has a style of his own and possesses a fluency of laeguage which stands him in good stead, but beyond quaintness and novelty the stories are far short of what they should be. There is a striking lack of plot and imaginative power and without these two elements a short story, no matter how nicely phrased, is colourless and weak. "Monsieur de Neron " has more in it of the dramatic than the others, but even this seems to be incomplete and lack a fitting de- wit:erne/7f. Whether Mr. Astor is striking out a ntw line of story-writing we do not know, but he would do better to keep to the old-fashioned type anti give what imagination and constructive ability be has full play. "Peace on earth and goodwiil to men " is the one universal precept at this time of the year, and on this ground perhaps there may be something to attract the reader in "Truths, New and Old," a volume of sermons by the Rev J M IVilson, M.A., vicar of Rochdale and Archdeacon of Manchester. (Archibald Constable St Co., Os.) The author seems to think it necessary to be a bit explanatory with regard o this volume and says," I am vicar of a church in whose porch I have placed a board with the Names and dates of all the vicars for 700 years. How greatly we should value a volume of ordinary parochial sermons preached by my predecessors of 100,200, 300 years ago in that pulpit! how inter- esting it would be to trace the continuity of sub- stance and the change of form!" A sounder reason is that which follows—that the younger clergy may learn the sort and range of sermons to which Lan- cashire working people will listen with interest and profit. The sermons range over a great variety of topics and are always clear in style while possessed of considerable eloquence which appears natural to the author. If they do not all convince their is ample food for thought in them and there purusal ought to be generally beneficial. "The Troubadour," a small volume of selections from English verse by Phillip Gibbs, is a very timely and pleasing production from the press of Messrs Cassell. We all know what a troubadour was in the ancient days—the thrice-welcome indi- vidual who with his harp slung on his back and his music in his wallet tarried at castle and cottage as the inclination moved him or tbe exigencies of travel ordained. His songs were of many kinds, of heroic deeds, the beauties of nature, the glories of

measure it performs the task undertaken by them. The poetry is of the best, the selections are admir- able, and wherever the volume finds its way it ought, and we believe will, be as welcome as the troubadour of old. It has lays and lyrics for monk moods, and fittingly at the present time in the forefront are placed the ballads God, of honour, courage, self-sacrifice and love, and they neanly always lingered long in the nieuiory of even the most uncultured hearers. Mr Gibbs' little volume has been celled" the Troubadour " as a tiny tribute to the old wandering minstrel, and in some which appeal to patriotism and loyalty. The suit- ability of the contents of .the book M. recitation should not be lost on either teachers or scholars, and, as the editor hopes, the learning by heart of noble poetry may be a cure for the cockneyism and slovenly pronunciation of the present day. The December number of the "Windsor Magazine " Once more eclipses all records. In addi- tion to a splendid supplement of a six-shilling novel. "Mistress Dorothy Marvin," by the popular author, J C Snaith, the magazine contains an unusually interesting pictorial feature, consisting of an apcount of Mr SE Waller and his work, profusely illustrated with reproductions, on special art paper, of his most famous canvases, such as "The Empty Saddle," "The Day of Reckoning," 'One and Twenty," and "The Challenge." Among the liter- ary contributors to this " marvellous shilling's worth." are Sir Edwin Arnold, H Rider Haggard, Guy Boothby, Israel Zangwill, E F Benson, Robert Barr, E Phillips Oppenlaeina, Barry Pain, and Halli- well Sutcliffe; while a new serial story, "The Whirligig," by Mayne Lindsay, is full of movement end excitement, and seems likely to prove one of the most striking books of the coming year. The artists employed on this magazine are as celebrate' . as the writers already quoted. " Cassell's Magazine "is well to the fore with a Christmas double number of surpassing excellenbe. Gratis with it is a handsome presentation pier e in Rernbraridt photogravure of Mr G Harcouree pic- ture "Good-bye," and several other choice pictures in similar style are interleaved with the fiction pages of the magazine. The numher contains the opening chapters of a new serial by the popular editor, Mr Max Pemberton, entitled " The Giant's Gate,' a tale oi Paris and embodying a scheme for the capture of London. Such swell-known character as General Mercier and oth3rs are introduced in the first in,talment which promises well for an excep- tionally powerful story. rialliwell Sutcliffa com- mences a series of exciting tales of Prince Cearlie's times and adventures, under the heading of " The White Cockade." Other contributors are Sir Walter Besant, Q., 8 R Crockett, E H Cooper, CV Godley, G Manville Fenn, Robert Barr, the late Stephen Crane Le Queux, T P O'Ooririor, M.P., and J Workman, The magazine is capitally illustrated throughout, and the important announcement is made that an entirely, new novel by Rudyard Kipling, entitled "Kim," will commence in the January number,and run throughout the year. " The Strand Magazsne " completes its tenth year with the December double number, aria Sir George Newnes has a timely greeting and word of thanks for the many friends of this excellent and world- wide periodical. An immense budget of literature it is, for the cover contains no less than '2OB pages and 250 illustrations, All for is. I Truly the period cm cheap literature is in its zenith. Would that it were all so excellent as the contents of the "Strand." There are contributions grave, gay, sensational and simple (these for the children) from L Meade and Robert Eustace, Alfred T SrorY, Harold White, James Workman, W W Jacobs, A E W Mason, John trange Winter, Richard Marsh, Roan Mee, and many others, Ti,e second instalment f H G Wells' serial " he First Men in the Moon " shows what a wonderful tale this imaginative writer has evolved, while among the articles, Ray Stannard Baker's description of the building of " The Deutschland" comes as an easy first "The Strand Magazine" is easily maintaining its great reputation, callicult as that must be.
