THE SEASON'S GREELINGS FROM THE QUEEN OF LUNA, FATHER CHRISTMAS, AND W. H. WATTS AND CO., LTD., COMPTON HOUSE

LIVERPOOL.

IT would be a pity to see Christmas-Boxes. all Christmas-boxes come to an end. They are .expressions of good will that cannot well be aban- doned. Kindliness between man and rnan,betvveen masters and servants, can seldom be wrong. They are evidences of pleasant ielations and bright human teasing. Had they been otherwise, they would not have persisted for so many hundreds of years, in the face of criticism, hard 'commercial- ism, and a rigid appraisement of values. But good things are constantly abused, and Christmas-boxes have suffered in c3mmon with other beneficent and wholesome things. They are sometimes accepted as a convenient way of supplementing small wages and salaries. Our postmen are allowed to collect Christmas-boxes, and they rely upon them to eke out poor wages. The same remark has to be made of club servants. To be candid, these are abuses, and we ought to be able to dispense with. them. Public services ought to ba remunerated properly and until they are paid better we shall have to bear, if we do not 'like, these charitable grants in aid of the Post Office, our clubs, and our municipal ser- vices. But there are even worse examples of the abuse of an old beneficence. The Christen- s-box has crept into business firms, until it has become quite an institution. Gifts are distributed at this time of the year with a punctiliousness and a liberality that makes them as much a part of busi- ness as anything else. If the gifts are not received the firm is likely to suffer, whatever may be its general liberality in rewarding service and in deal- ing with buyers and customars on enlightened principles. The Christmas-box, instead of being a personal kindliness, has become a disguised busi- ness commission. Large limited liability firms are divided into several departments, and the heads of these departments are in expectation of receiving presents, either in money or in kind, from the wholesale firms with which they do business. Some hundreds of pounds are disbursed by manufac- turers, agents, and dealers in this way. They create goodwill, but it is goodwill of a commercial nature. More orders are expected, a less critical examination of goods supplied is looked for, and a bias is intended to follow which is being paid for as much as if a bargain were made over a counter or an writing. The money so expended comes out of the firm whose servants are ,the recipients of these gifts. It does not mean a reduction in the profits of the gift-giving firms. All the vice of commission-giving lies perdu in the Christmas- box. It means that a bigger, profit must be made on the goods that are sold. It amounts to a bribe, without the moral shock that attends an open -bribe. Accordingly, it is to be condemned,and we are delighted to see that a practical move has boon made against the :system, even though the cynics may see in the reform another effort in the direc-

tion of coercement to obtain b3tter dividends. The false Christmas-box is not to be defended on the ground that it is an imitation of the true one. The true one is personal, and free from the taint of commercialism. The false one has little or nothiLg of the personal element in it. It is mechanical, expectant, demoralising. It is like betting on horses never seen, through a hook- ‘maker who may never attend the race. Any check to its domination, however small, is a moral gain.

CAUTION is an excellent The War Not Over. quality where unfinished

Campaigns are concerned, and it is well not to halloo before you are out of the wood. We seems to have been much too pre- vious in several of our arrangements. The C.l.V.'s were sent home before their services could be dis- pensed with, and several bodies of Colonial troops have been permitted to return before the war has •ended. That end has been proclaimed more than once,and:the home-coming of Lord ROBERTS seems to have been premature, as well as the thanks- giving services, which have now been abandoned. The war is not over, and we have daily evidence of the fact. The Boers are acting upon a plan which seems to have been carefully thought out, and to be dimly discernible in recent operations. DE WET intended to carry the campaign into the north of Cape Colony,which has been again invaded, in the hope of rallying to his aid the discontented spirits and the ardent sympathisers who have been 'inflamed by imaginary accounts of events in

England and atrocities in the field. The present state of the conflict has been described as a guerilla war. It is something more than that when British positions can be stormed and hun- dreds of British soldiers taken prisoners, if they have been immediately released at soon as they have been stripped of arms and ammunition. The audacity of the Boers in making attaCks upon our positions has besi rewarded with a large amount of success. The old carelessness seems to have returned. Our men occupy good :positions, but they do not entrench themselves or build schanzes, or take care to command the lines of approach by guns and Maxims. Our sentry arrangements are bad, because they are not pushed out far enough at night, when there is now evidently sonic danger of attack. The arrangements for signalling are imperfect. The rocket has been virtually aban- doned, though it must often be valuable in the case of night advances, or as an indication that the enemy is in the immediate neighbourhood of our camps. The telegraph arrangements are not always satisfactory. DELA.REY was in regular receipt, it is said, of the messages from Pretoria which were passing to General CLEMENTS and other British generals in the field,' owing to the wire having been left intact. We have much to learn in slimness from the enemy, not only in scouting bit in destroying ammuni_ tion we cannot carry away. Our recent opera- tions have been governed by the idea that the Boers had no plan, and so we have no sooner cleared off it district than an enemy reappears where he had been least expected. It seems that we are always anticipating a collapse whick never comes, or proclaiming that DE WET or somebody else is surrounded, only to discover that the hope is false, and the escape is almost certain. It is not easy to conduct wfdely-scattered operations from a given centre, but it is clear that the Boers are doing something of the kind, with less scientific means of intercommunication than wie are supposed to possess. Let us dismiss all our illusions, and reconcile ourselves to the fact that the war is not over, and will not be over any sooner by reason of our haste to indulge in cele- brating success and to weaken our forces in the field. It is a sorry business to have to check a nation's desires. But it is laer to tell the truth, and to settle down to face an ugly and a most dis- agreeable situation.

Where does the mistletoe come from which at this season of the year is sold for the decoration of our rooms, and to provide innocent amusement for our young people? The prevailing impres- sion i s that it is stripped from the branches of ancestral oaks in English woods, whose predeces- sors, saw the long-haired Druids gather round for the performance of their mystic rites. As a matter of fact, the supply which is sold in London and in other large towns comes chiefly from Nor- mandy, where it is carefully cultivated for trade purposes, the greater quantity going to London, notwithstanding the fact that a higher price is often to be obtained in Paris. It is worthy of mention that among other,custorners is the United States, where some who still celebrate Christmas in the old-fashioned manner are willing to pay a very heavy price for apiece of mistletoe. 'As for the holly which is offered in our markets and shops, that, of course, is home grown, with the exception of a small amount from Guernsey; but as to its precise place of origin there may be even greater mystety. Much of it is sent up honestly by farmers who trim their hedges at this season of the year, and send up truckloads for what it will fetch. But doubtless great quantities are ob- tained by persons who make no pretence of obtain- taining the permission of the original owner,taking a licence which goes sotnewhat beyond even the special privileges of the festive season. It is also found that at Christmas time young firs are fre- quently missing from plantations; but these facts derogate nothing from the trade in holly and Christmas trees. If all the pheasants and rabbits of our poulterers' shops could tell the story of their capture, what work there might be for the police!

If there is any truth in the statement that a green Christmas means a fat churchyard, it may be partly due to the custom which requires roar- ing tires at this period of the year, whatever may be the general temperature of the air. In warm- ing appliances the houses of the British middle class are badly equipped indeed as compared with those of a similar class on the Continent, and such apparatus as is provided is not always used with the greatest discretion. Similarly, in the waiting- rooms of railway stations, and, ad a correspondent mentions, in Zirailway carriages themselves, the unintelligent use of heating stoves in mild weather may be a fertile cause of severe cold, and even of more serious ailments. The corres- pondent speaks of experiencing a temperature of a hundred degrees- Fahrenheit on a journey on a line unnecessary to specify. Such a heat, whether of the "dry, prickly kind" of which he complains or of the moist variety, cannot but be injurious 'of itself, and no ventilation is possible except by the opening of a window, from which under the cir- cumstances, i particularly deadly draught is to be expected. Experiment has shown that the human frame is capable of sustaining for a very short period heats which very nearly approach the actual roasting point. Such martyrdoms, however, not so much in the cause of science as to gratify scien- tific curiosity, should be left to voluntary sufferers. Even a certain degree of cold, if it has not the tonic effect which was once ascribed to it, is better than the over-heating of which the correspondent referred to complains.

The annual report of the Warrington Savings Rink is a record of continued progress and pros- perity. Daring the year no fewer than 789 new or renewed accounts have been °pelted, bringing up the total number of depositors to 12,636, and the balance due to them stands at £353,4133 3s 7d. This speaks volumes for the thriftiness of the people in this town and district. The Warrington Savings Bank is carried on for the special purpose of assisting those who are anxious to provide for the future. The man who has only a spare shilling is welcomed as a depositor, and he can start an aecount with that amount. It is in this respect that tho Savings Bank differs from the aims of ordinary banks. Banking companies as a rule desire to make a profit by facilitating business, but the Savings Bank seeks first and foremost to it s success thrift, and the measure of success is gauged by the large number of people it can induce to make deposits. The large number of small depositors is a distinct feature of the Warrington Savings Bank business. At the meeting on Tuesday it was reported that there were 5,163 depositors who had invested amounts which did not exceed a sovereign. This proves conclusively that the bank is resorted to by people receiving small wages but who nevertheless are desirous of having some sav- ings to fall back upon. In all probability if the ironworkers who are now so hard up had exercised the thrift of people earning far less money per week, there would have been no appeal this Christmas on their behalf. We recommend the Savings Bank to their consideration.

Interest in matters pertaining to cricket is not very keen at this time of the year, but the action of the County captains in debarring Arthur Mold from F rst-class matches has creatAd quite a sensa- tion, and particularly in Lancashire. For a dozen years or more Mold has been the mainstay of the Lancashire attack, and for the County to be thus suddenly deprived of his services is a very serious matter indeed. Mold was—though not to the same extent as Briggs—ono of the idols of the crowd, and at Oki Trafford he will be very much missed. His delivery has always been considered suspicious by those who pose as judges of bowling, and, when Phillips no-balled him last season there was an indication that something

THE EX.A.MINER, FRIDAY, DECEMBER 21, 1900 I MATTERS OF MOMENT. !RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

was in the wind. Personally we never saw the Lancashire fast bowler send down a ball which appeared to be thrown, and we think his short run and quick delivery have much to do with the suspicion which has gathered ground during the last few seasons. It is hard luck for both the player and his county. but the former has the con- solation of his £2,000 "benefit" last seuon. The action of the authorities is, however, to be heartily commended. They have resolutely cackled an evil which had some tendency to spread, and in dealing drastically with those men wliom they honestly believe to he unfair bowlers they are rendering a great service to the summer game.

An unfortunate dispute has arisen at the works of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Limited. A number of boilermen and firemen have been dis- missed, or as trades unionists prefer to call it "locked out." The case for the m.3n was fully laid before a large meeting on Sunday evening, and Messrs. Crosfield's rejoinder will be found in another column. The dispute resembles in some respects that of the Peurhyn quf,rrymen. It was contended at the meeting that Messrs. Crosfield's action was an attempt to interfere with the rights of combination, but from the facts which have been made public, it would be more correct to say that it is an objection on the part of the firm to accept the assistance or brook the interference of a trades union official in the settlement of a grievance. That is one of the chief points at issue in he Penrhyn dispute.. Why there should be this diffidence to discuss trade matters with a union official, who, after all is the best representa- tive of the men, we are at a loss to understand. Many manufacturers have given testimony to the valuable assistance which trades union officials have been able to give in bringing about an amic- able settlement of trade grievances. One of Messrs Crosfield's chief competitors in the soap trade has not only suffered the "interference" of the official whom Messrs Crosfield refuse to recog- nise but a set of working conditions has been mutually drawn up and is now strictly observed byjboth sides. Messrs. Crosfield prefer to deal directly with their employees but it is a well- known fact that workmen dislike to approach em- ployers with reference to grievances, for, rightly or wrongly, they are of opinion that those who voice them are made to suffer in various ways for their outspokenness. The question in dispute seems such a small matter that two good business men with proper notions of fairness ought to be able to settle the dispute in half an hour.

If the Corporation are not, " like Alexander, sighing for other worlds to conquer, they are very anxious to replace Warrington Bridge with an entirely new and really adequate structure as soon as possible. The need is groat, in fact it is little use spending thougands of pounds in widening Bridge-street if the Bridge is to remain in the posi- tion of the neck to the bottle. A new bridge of the kind required is, however, rather too big an undertaking for the Corporation to attempt single- handed, and it will be very unfair if assist- ance is not forthcoming from the two County Councils directly concerned in the improvement of the existing means of communication netween Lancashire and Cheshire. In the past they have been very stubborn and close-fisted in the matter but last week overtures were made to the authori- ties of the County Palatine which may, it is sin- cerely hoped, result in something encouraging if not entirely satisfactory. Warrington's call on the County Councils is a very modest one and if they would only contribute the comparatively trifling sum of £3,000 each it is more than likely that the Warrington Corporation would accept the responsi- bility of providing a new bridge. We sincerely hope that the stubborn authorities will presently adopt that attitude of sweet reasonableness which would be so much more becoming and not delay such a vast and imperative improvement.

A Provincial Letter.

I At the close of a record making and a record breaking century it is but fitting, perhaps, that the very last month should establish a record for itself of some kind. To the middle of the month, and since, the indications have been that the last Chris- mas of the century was not to be" old-fashi sued," from the weather point of view. Tee atmospheric conditions have been persistently miid; a state of things not confined to one part of the country only, but common throughout the British Isles. To such a degree has this been observable that, according to meteorologists, the mildness of the month has stamped it in this respect a record December. The supply of air being drawn off the warm Atlantic has caused appearances to be somewhat out of har- mony with the kind of festivity that usually signalises Christmastide. True, we shall nave the holly and the mistletoe just the same, but the snow and the crisp frost appear at the time of writing very unlikely to ia;leupy their accustotned places as accessories Nevertheless, those who are charitably disposed will have learned by this time, from the multitude of appeals in the papsrs, that the great festival of the year is near at band once again. it may be, in one respect, that the pheno- menally open weather of the season will militate against the occurrence of exceptional distress, inas- much as damp does not preclude the pursuit of certain kinds of outdoor work as hard frost and snow do. But, though poverty may remain noi- mal, there is always at this time of the year suffi- cient to stimulate the wealthy, the strong, and the benevolent to the performance of good works. It is only the hardened cynic who would say in answer to the special Christmastide appeals that relief under the poor-law should suffice. But even a re- vised poor-law system will never be held by people generally in this country as sufficient amelioration of the lot of tha indigent, and the better-lisposed among us will always go on giving at Christmastide.

• Three days hence thousands of re-united fanailie4, scattered over the country, will surround the festive board. The son who twelve months since was fight- ing his country's battles under the burning sun of South Africa, will by his presence, again gladden the home of his parents. The husband, returned invalided from the front,with his children clinging to his knee, will recall the weary marches, and the short rations, of his last Christmas. How marked the contrast between then and now ! Nor will the children in our hnspitals,or the" mites" of the very poor, be forgotten this Christmas time.

The Lancet points out that the overwork so com- mon just before Christmas is not healthy, and that anything that can help to give shopkeepers and their assistants sufficient leisure to recoup from the exhaustion of their daily avocation, is ot public benefit: With all of which the public will agree. In the United States some ladies appear t) have taken a generous:initiative in this matter, and have formed what they term the " Consumers' League," the members of which pleige themselves not to make purchases after four o'clock in VW after- noon, and not to allow parcels to he sent to their houses after six o'clock. This may not be practic- able in England,and particularly in provincial manufacturing towns, end at Christmas time; but there is no doubt that a great deal of the rush which is Irma!, at this season could be prevented if ladies and others exercised a little more thought and consideration for tradespeople and their helpers.

A very important question was raised by a deputation from the Chambers,of Commerce of the United Kingdom which waited up3n the Board of Trade the other das, and one that has been farther considered in Birmingham during the preient week. The objec, was to obtain the good oftle,s of the Trade Department in the promotion of improved methods of using the water communica- tions of the provinces for commercial purposes, and also to set on foot some system of development which would enable the fullest We to be made of our resources in this direction. It is, of course, desirable that our canals and rivers should be turned, as far as convenient, to the purpose of limiting the monopoly of the railways. This indeed, has been one of their principal uses in the past, and there was a certain amount of satisfaction to be derived from the official statement indicating that instead of a decrease, there hips been a slight increase, in the last ten years,in the employ- ment of canals, and in the mileage of these water- ways. There has likewise been sOine falling-off in the number of canals acquired by the railway com- panies. At the same time, it is in no way sugges- ted that the deputation, or the movement of which it was the mouthpiece, was in any sense hostile to the railways. The deputation suggested the appointment of a Royal Commission, but to this the President of the Board of Tale demurred, on the ground that, although the importance of the question was great, a sufficiently urgent case had not been made out for invoking th machinery indicated. But it is not expeCtedP that the matter will be allowed to drop. The Chamber of Commerce, at any rate, propose to strike while the iron is hot. Bristol Corporation is in the meantime endeavouring to influence that of Birmingham in a movement for improving tha waterway communication between the Midlands and the British Channel; but the former cannot be said, as yet, to bays received much encouragement. Yet Birmingham is glorying in the thought of be- coming like Liverpool, a seaport.

A glance at some of the provincial police courts during the week demonstrates nnmistakably how much reason there was for Lord Roberts's appeal to the public against the indiscriminate treating o f soldiers returned from the front. On Monday quite a batch of absent-minded beggars had at one court to suffer the indignity of fin-s, and one the aldi- tional blackmark of imprisonment. At another, where the name deserves mention—Evesham—a re- cord of eleven general engagements and twenty- three minor affairs did not prevent a conviction being recorded for obscene language. The facts are not creditable, and tend to show the neeecsity, with Christmas at hand, of the Comm tnder-in- Chiers warning being kept in mind.

" Chemist " wrote the other day, saying that in the midst of discussion as to the amount of arsenic there may he present in beer, attention had not been called to the amount of glucose there is to be found in the cheaper kinds of sweets, notably " peppermint rock." The inevitable result, as in the case of beer, is the appearance of letters from manu- facturers of sweetmeats, claiming absolute purity for their own goods respectively. The Local Govern- ment Board urge that nat only beer, but sweets, syrups, and other manufactures should be analysed, and authorities having to al minister the Food and Drugs Act are being moved to action by Parish and Urban District. Councils in the matter, while some have adopted resolutions in support of Sir Cuthbert Quiltet's Pure Beer Bill. It F1,P111.4 as if some of the local authorities will make things lively on these questions after Christmas is over.

'APerho.ps one of the most interesting features of this fag-end of the century is the vitality of our sc- called old men. It is not too much to say that there ar, comparatively few old men at the present day, as the term was once understood. It is true that they may he grey-haired, and seem old, but they are not really ancient. Not very long ago, the man who was over seventy thought it right and proper to sit in an easy chair by the corner of the fire, and study serious literature for the remainder of his days. But at the present time these men are much more aggressive. They very properly decline to believe in the necessity for laying-un, and the con- sequence is that there are very many men of mature age about who eau well hold their OWII in the activi- ties of life with their younger competitors. A glance at the ages of our Generals in the South African campaign, and in other directions, shows that they possess all the experience of a ripe age, combined with the brightness of youth. We are delighted to see it, and heartily wish that centen- arians may multiply.

ANTINACCINATIONIST AT WIDNES.

Before the Widnes magistrates on Monday William Wilkinson, 87, Mersey-road, succeeded in getting an exemption order from vaccination. Applicant made an unsuccesstul attempt on Mon- day last to convince the Mayor that he had a con- ocientious belief, and stated on Monday morning that they had no right to refuse him what the strongest Government of the last fifty years had granted. He was the father of thirteen children, and he never did and never would believe in vaccin- ation.—The Mayor said he thought be had been wrongly advised, and, in granting the application, said the law with regard to vaccination was vary ' bad. The applicant left the court beaming with smiles, and evidently delighted with his victory gained for the cause of anti-vaccination. ACCIDENT AT THE BOTTOM LOCK, RUNCORN.

On -aturday night, about six o'clock, an accid- ent occurred at the Bottom Lock, which opens into the Ship Canal. It appears that the steam barge Forth, belonging to the Rochdale Canal Co., had en- tered the lock from the Ship Canal, and, fullavin4 the usual custom, steps were taken to lift the small cock boat attached to the barge on to the quay side Thomas Banner,the engineer of the barge, got on to the quay side to put the crane in motion, and as he was looking down into the lock to see it there was enough chain out, the crane was moved round by the force of the wind, and his fingers were trapped in the cog wheels. He was extricated and taken to Dr. Robinson's surgery in an exhausted condition owing to the loss of blood. An examina- tion disclwed the fact that several fingers were smashed. The spot where the accident occurred is vary bwily lighted, and in the darkness it was not possible to see the crane moving. Banner, who lives in Hartley-street, will lose several ifingers as the result of the accident. CHILD BURNING FATALITY AT WIDNES.

Mr. Rrighnuse held an inquest a the Accident Hospital, Widnes, on Monday, on the body of Margaret Woods, aged six years, who met her death from severe burns on Friday morning, The mother Martha Woods, residing at 35, Charlotte-street, said she left the deceased dni. and another child, aged two years, by themselves. There was a small tire in the grate. About ten o'clock Su 4anne Folding heard some screams, and on going into the street found the girl a mass of flames. She wrapped a mat round her, by which the flames were extinguished. Susanne Golding corroborated the foregoin4 state- ment. Martha Warburton, matron, said the child was admitted on Friday morning,and died the same afternoon. The Coroner said a mother's first duty was to her children, and she had no business to leave them by themselves. A verdict of" Accidental death" was returned. GROSS NEGLECT OF CHILDREN' •AT WIDNES.

At the Widnes Police Court, on Wednesday, before the Mayor (Alderman G I Neil) and Mr. B Brown, John Reynolds and Margaret Reynolds, Water - street, were charged with neglecting their family, namely, Joseph, aged twelve; Edward, ten; James, eight : Mary, four; and a baby, seven months, on the Bth December and on divers other dates. Mr Knowles. solicitor, presecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.O C. Alter police evidence had been heard, Inspector Hackett, N.8.P.C.C., said he hal had the case under notice for some time. On the 18th December he visited the house and found the woman under the Influence of drink. There was no food in the house with the exception of half-a-pound of bread. The upstairs portion was in a disgraceful state. He had seen better pigsties. The furniture was very scanty, most of the things being in the hands of the pawnbroker. The bed was in a filthy condition, and the children practically slept on the iron rods—John Reynolds, who pleaded not guilty, said he was not to blame. He worked hard, and gave his wife his money, She usually spent it in drink. The Mayor characterised the case as a very painful one which had given them much anxiety. There was only one way out of the difficulty. He hoped the lesson they would have to learn would make them more careful in future. Tney would each be sent to goal for one month with hard labour.

SWELLING THE MILLION GUINEAS FUND

The quarterly meeting of the Runcorn iVesleyan Circuii- was held in the Guild Parlour, Brunswick School, on Monday. The Rev A Wells occupied the chair. The chief item of business was a resolution, proposed by Mr A R Norman, seconded by Mr R Posnet, to raise 1,600 guineas, instead of the 1,330 guineas originally promises, towards the million- guineas scheme. It will be remembered that the Liverpool district, in which Runcorn is included, decided, at an early date, to raise 50,000 guineas; but at the conference last year, it was resolved, if possible to make that sum into 70,000 guineas. This amount, with the exception of a little over 13,000 has been raised, and therefore with a view of rais- ing the additional sun required, the Chairman of the District has written to the off districts asking what promises they could offer towards making up the amount. The Runcorn Wesleyaus have unani- mously decided to add 270 guineas to the amount they had already promised, making a total for the Runcorn Circuit of 1,600 guineas, instead of the 1,330 promised last year. It is interesting to note that at the meeting on Monday evening no less than 135 guineas towards this additional call was volun- tarily subscribed by those present. The financial positions of the circuit is also considerably better than it has been for some time past,a deficit having been wipod off by the proceeds of the lecture de- livered by the Rev A Wells on "Pompeii" some time ago. A SAILOR MISSING—:STRINGE CASE.

Paul Jeay, a native of Jersey, Channel Isles, A.B. on the schooner, "Lancashire uad,.' disappeared un- der somewhat mysterious circumstances on Tuesday evening. The vessel arrived in the tidal basin that day, aad Jeay and a shipmate named George Wilson spent several hours in Runcorn on Tuesday after- noon en3oying themselves. They returned to the ship at tea time, and Wilson who was departing by train obtained his bag. Jeay was to accompany him to the starioa. Wilson left the vessel first, and was waking along the quay side, expecting Jeay to follow him when he heard Jetty cry out "George! G-eorga! " but when he turned round Jeay was no- where to be seen and there was no disturbance of the water in the dock. It was however felt certain that he had fallen into the water and been drowned, air: information reaching the police station, Superintendent Pearson bad the dock dragged with grapnels for several hours without result. On Wednesday morning Mr W Shaw, the seamen's missionary, resumed the search with his patent grapnels, but up to evening the body of the missing man had not been recovered.

THE BEER QUESTION.

The Local Government B )+rd forwarded a circular letter on the questionl of arsenic in beer With- out expressing any opinion on the question but wi'h the view of pointing out a means of discovering whether any risk is likely to arise in the district from the use of beer containing injurious ingrrdients,the Board directed attention to the powers which the Council possessed under the Sale of Food and Drugs Acts, of purchasing and submitting samples of beer for analysis be the Pubic Analyst, and to recommend that samples sly uld be tak'n forthwith. Glucose and other s :gar substitutes were used not only in the breo ing of beer, but also in the making of jams, syrups, sweets and similar artic e; of food, and the Board suggested for the consideration of the Council whether it would not be well that samples of some or all of such articles which are on sale in ths district should be purchased and submitted to the Public Analyst for analyss. The Board were informed that there was no difficulty in ascertaining whether beer or other articles of food contain arsenical poison. The Clerk explained that the County Council were taking and analysing samples all over the county, and if the Di trict Council did anything they would most likely only clash. He would suggest that the matter be lett over until he had written, to the County Council And ascertained how far they effected their district. (Hear, hear.)

Dear Sirs—l reside in Cheshire and lamin a difficulty respecting a nuisance arising from some modern method introduned by the local tannery. RUNCORN NOT ES

[By a Correspondent].

Within the past few days Mr Lightburn clerk to ' the Runcorn School Board, has placed in the hands of the members an interesting document - with regard to the attendance at public ele entery schools in the town. It appears that the average number of children on the school registers during the past year was 3,402 and the average. attendance was 2,896 ; an increase of 41 on the previous year. The number of parents prosecuted before the magis- trates was 171, an increase of 35 over the previoug year. The highest percentage of attendance was at Victoria-road Board School namely 92.1. National School boys coming next with 805. The percent- age of attendance throughout including the boat children who are ever a source of anxiety and worry was 85.1 and is the highest hitherto recorded In the previous year the percentage was 84.9. rhis increased average attendance has came ! in govern- ment grants about .C6l 103 more than last year. The number of children absent every time the schools were opened was 506, one less than the previous year and as Mr Light- burn neatly phrases it "these figures show the regularity with which the irregular children do not attend school." This great evil, continued Mr Lightburn, is chiefly due to parental neglect, and making allowanee for excusable causes, has pro- duced a loss in Government grants to the schools in Runcorn of £558 10,i. It is anticipated that the increase in the maximum fine for non-attendance from 5s to 20s will have a beneficial effect upon neglectful parents. Compared with the rest of the country the average attendance in Runcorn is very good.

Apropos jof Education, Mr. J. M. Williams,chair- man of Runcorn Board of Guardians, mentioned a small grievance of parents at the meeting on Tues- day. It is, however, the little things that tell in this life. Mr. Williams pointed out that it was a hardship upon poor parent,' to compel them to pay for a doctor's certificate when a child is ill To a person with well-lined pockets 6d is a matter of no moment, but to a father earning perhaps 17s per week and a large family to keep 6d represents a very serious drain. Mr. Williams suggested that as education was compulsory, provision ought to be made for the payment of doctot's certificat' 83nd be asked the members to consider the question with a view of taking some action.

Ere another issue of the " Examiner," Christmas will have come and gone and I take this oppor- tunity of wishing the readers of this column a Merry Christmas and a Happy New Year. The holi- days this year will be fairly extensive, At most works,work will be su-pended at noon on Saturday and not resumed until Thursday morning.

A CHRISTMAS BETROTHAL.

BY CLA R A 11 ',HOLLAND,

Author of "A Fats" Step," "The Mystery of Abbeylands," "Two Against One," "Muriere Faith," "The Strange Disaupearance of Lance Martin," &c.

Verona awoke one morning, and, in a dreamy, half-pleased, half-sad fashion, remembered that she was eighteen. She yawned, stretched herself, and sat up lazily. It was late, quite ten o'clock, yet her maid had not come in to pull up the blinds or bring her her early cup of tea. She had been warned that this would happen, so was not surprised. It was her duty to sleep as long as possible, for she was to go to her first ball that night. Her heart danced, her cheeks glowed at the thought, and she sprang out of bed. Further rest was out of the question. To lie there was only so much waste of time. She felt she must be up and doing on this the first day of her young lady- hood. She threw her pretty pink dressing-gown round her shoulders, thrust her feet into her slippers, and tripping across the floor drew aside the curtain and stood, her elbows on the window- sill, looking out. The scene before her was a charming one. The old gardens below were covered in snow. The trees and. shrubs, white with frost and rime, sparkled and shone, sending forth countless iridescent colours in the sunshine.

Across the lawn, making his way with some difficulty through the deep snow, walked a tall, dark-haired man of four or five and twenty, a gun upon his shoulder, a dog at his heels. _ _ _

With a blush and a smile Verona withdrew hastily. As she did so he raised his head, and his eyes rested for a moment upon the window of her room. Then he whistled to the little terrier and tramped on. From her post behind the curtain Verona watched him, a smile upon her lips, a gleam of admiration in her eyes. "Dear, kind Fred," she sighed, as he dis- appeared, hidden from her sight by a big clump of snow-topped rhododendrons. "There's no one like him, so good, so true, so noble, so handsome. And yet what a poor position is his; my father's agent, a man bound to work for his living, and that but a paltry one. No one would hear of it— if he—if I—i f we were both so .foolish as to— But there, it cannot be. I could not endure it. I am ambitious and require more than he can give. Of what use to be beautiful only to love and marry a poor agent? As the Countess of Minton! Ah, that is what will suit me."

She took up a hand-mirror from the dressing- table and examined herself with extreme satisfac- tion. Her hair was like spun gold, and fine, soft, and abundant. Her cheeks and lips were pink; her eyes a light blue, darkly shaded by thick black lashes; her brows well-marked yet daintily pencilled. She was tall and slender, and carried herself with the air of a princess.

Neither Verona nor her friends could tell whether her beauty and refinement were not more a matter of accident than a right of inheritance; for, as far as anyone knew, she was not entitled, either by birth or breeding, to the marvellous loveliness and look of distinction for which she had been remarkable even in her babyhood. Be that as it may, these graces had decided her fate, and placed her in the luxurious home in which we find her at the beginning of this story.

Sixteen years before, whilst travelling in Italy Mr. and Mrs. Norman Fair broke their journey at Verona. By chance they put up at a small hotel in a quiet and almost unfrequented part of the quaint old town. Here Mrs. Fair fell ill, and during her convalescence she formed a deep attach- ment to a tiny girl, who every morning toddled into her room to offer her a few roses or a bouquet of sweettscented lilies. The graceful little creature completely won her heart. She had never seen a child so lovely, and when she found that she was only a waif, kept by the landlord out of charity, she implored her husband to allow her to adopt her and take her home. Greatly pleased with the little one himself, Mr. Fair agreed, on condition that there were no impecunious relatives likely to turn up to claim her later on, or worry him for money. The landlord assured him that such a thing was impossible. The child had been left to his care by a young woman to whom he had given shelter one wild winter's night. She was ill when he admitted her to the hotel, and had died the next morning. She was an American, the widow of a courier dead some six months before. She begged him to be kind to her baby, declaring she had not a friend in the world to whom she could send it, and he had promised to do what he could for her helpless infant. "So far she has been no trouble," he said. "The chambermaids love and look after her. " However, I cannot keep her much longer, If Signora Fair adopts her, well and good; if not she must go to an orphanage."

At this Mrs. Fair cattght the baby to her breast and vowed she would never give her up. She had no child of her own, and had longed for one day and night. It would make her very happy to adopt this lovely little girl.

Her husband said she might do as she pleased, and so the matter was settled.

A few weeks later the Fairs set out for England, accompanied by a picturesque-looking Italian nurse and the pretty child, whom Mrs. Fair had decided was to be henceforth called by the fanciful name Verona.

The Fairs were wealthy people, and their home in Buckinghamshire was one of the finest old houses in the county. But as their wealth had been but recently acquired and in trade, their position was a little uncertain, their standing in the nighbour- hood not at all what they desired. In beautiful Glastonwold, surrounded by every luxury, pampered and petted to her heart's content, the little waif had grown and developed into a lovely maiden. In all the countryside there was no one to equal her in beauty and accomplish- ments; and as she emerged from childhood and was seen riding and dtiving through the pretty lanes, or visiting the poor people in the village, many admiring eyes were turned upon her. Every- one asked her name and wanted to know her. And when at last it was rumoured that the stately and exclusive Countess of Minton with her son, the Earl, had called and paid frequent visits at Glastonwold, every lady of any importance for miles round found that it was her duty to do like- wise. And so, before Verona had left the school- room she had received adulation enough to turn an older and a wiser head, and was fully convinced that the first thing necessary for happiness was to be beautiful, the second, to marry well. She was a nobody she knew; but her adopted father had promised her a large fortune, and the world was at her feet. So she determined to make the most of her opportunities, and, putting aside all ideas of love and liking, marry the man who could best satisfy her ambition by placing .her in an undoubtedly high and enviable position. These doctrines had been constantly instilled into her mind from her earliest years by her adopted parents, and so completely did she appear to have imbibed them that they never imagined for a moment that she wotild ever be induced to swerve from them by any feeling or consideration. Mr. and Mrs. Fair were devoted to the lovely girl. She had given them entire satisfaction, and they looked upon her as their own child. They loved her very dearly, and were not too proud to acknowledge the benefit they had reaped from her presence in their home. Thanks to her, they had won their way into the good graces of their neigh- bours, and because of her, they were invited to all the best houses in the county. A great future was in store for Verona, they told themselves, and they spared neither. trouble nor expense in their anxiety to show her off and give her every advantage and opportunity. Full of admiration for the girl's beauty and marvellous powers of fascination, they were not surprised, therefore, when, some six weeks before her eighteenth birthday, the Earl of Minton made her a formal offer of tuaeriage. Ho was a man of the world; not particularly agreeable, and some forty years of age. But he was an earl, and, though deeply in debt, owner of a fine house and a good property. So they welcomed him gladly as a suitor for beautiful Verona ; and, looking upon her as a most fortunate young person, rejoiced openly when she agreed to become his wife.

"I am really pleased," Mrs. Fair remarked to her husband when it was all settled and she had seen Verona off for a walk round the park with her betrothed, "for although the sweet girl is so sensible, I was beginning to fear Fred Constantine's influence. He is handsome, and they have always been so much together." Her husband laughed. "What a romantic

Not wishing to thwart her unnecessarily, they consented to keep the matter secret, and things went on much as usual at Glastonwold.

The ball to be given in honour of Verona's birth- day, a few nights before , Christmas, was to be a big affair, an entertainment the like of which had never been seen in the neighbourhood before. Everything was to be done on the most splendid scale. The music, supper, wine, lights and flowers, were to be as perfect as London and money could produce. The house was filled with visitors. Lord Minton and his mother, and a host of people of his set, introduced to the Fairs by him, and known to them only as his friends, filled the best rooms, and made themselves quite at home. The place rang with life and merriment from morning till night, and the revels were expected to last till after Christmas. As Verona laid down the mirror with a well- pleased smile, and sank into the depths of a luxurious armchair, the door opened and her maid entered the room. In one hand she carried a large bouquet of exquisite exotics, from which hung long streamers of white satin ribbon, the ends edged with silver fringe. To one of these Lord Minton's card was fastened by a diamond brooch. "There," cried Selina, holding out the bouquet, "isn't that superb ? " Verona glanced at it carelessly. "Very. From Covent Garden, I suppose." "Straight. His lordship watched Richards un- pack it, and pinned his card, on himself—with this." "Ah !" The girl took the brooch, and, looking at it with sparkling eyes, fastened it in amongst the lace upon her dressing-gown. "This is nice. Put the bouquet in a vase in the boudoir till the evening." "And these ? " Selina drew her other hand from behind her back, and held out a few white roses and maidenhair, lightly flung together and tied with a silken thread, to which was attached a slip of paper, with these words written upon it in a clear hand:

Many happy returns of the day. —FR."

"How- sweet-I" Verona raised-the blossoms to ner lips. "How deliciously fresh and simple. Grown in the hothouse at Melrose, I daresay."....

"Yes, miss; tended and looked after by Mr. Constantine himself. Shall I put them in the boudoir too, miss ? " "No. I'll do so presently myself. Be quick, Selina, and bring me my breakfast. I want to go out; and be careful of that bouquet—it is very costly." "Indeed it is—and beautiful. It will look lovely with your dress to-night, miss," and the maid hurried away. That afternoon the Glastonwold house-party spent several hours skating upon the big lake at the bottom of the park. The ice was perfect, and the scene was a brilliant and lively one; the bright drebses of the happy young skaters as they went swiftly to and fro standing out in picturesque relief against' the dazzling whiteness of the surrounding snow. The merriest and most gracefully energetic of the many charming girls was Verona—Verona in crimson velvet and sables, a small feather-trimmed toque upon her golden hair. The Earl of Minton was not present. He had been called to London that morning on urgent business, and went away full of regret, but promising to return without fail in time for the ball in the evening. Disappointed for a moment, Verona soon recovered her usual spirits. As she sat down near the lake to have her skates nut on, Fred Constantine sprang forward, and with an eager "Allow me," dropped on one knee before her, and with quick and dexterous fingers fastened every buckle securely. He was a tall, handsome fellow, and an excellent skater, and many envious, though perforce admir- ing eyes followed him as, taking Verona's little gloved hand within his own, he swung along with her across the ice. The hours flew past like moments, and when tea- time came round they left the ice sadly, and walked silently back to the house together. "You will wear my flowers to-night, Verona?" he said, in a low voice, as they parted at the door. "Just one or two in token of our friendship." "I will," she cried, impulsively, hardly knowing what she said, her lovely eyes upon his face, the soft colour coming and going in her cheeks, "and no others. You are my best—my oldest friend." And then, turning away, she ran into the house. "Oh, if I dared think, dared hope," murmured Fred Constantine, as he went with unsteady feet over the frozen ground. "But it is—it would be— folly. And yet, my darling, I would make you happier than any man amongst them, no matter how great." That night, when Verona, a radiant vision in white satin and tulle, her only ornament a bunch of small white roses, glided softly into the ball- room, Lord Minton hurried to her side.

"How lovely!" he whispered. "One could fancy you a creature from the other world." . .

Verona laughed gaily, and made him a sweeping curtsey. "Thank you. I prefer to belong to this world—just at present." "I should say so. But "—his eyes wandering from her lovely face to her slim, lithe figure, in its simple yet elegant attire—" why do you not carry my bouquet? Why do you not wear my brooch ? " "Diamonds are not for the young," she replied, with an airy wave of her hand; "and a bouquet would hamper my movements and weary me. I am going to dance, not to pass my time as a bouquet holder. But "—seeing a gathering frown upon his brow—"your bouquet is beautiful; and I have it and the brooch side by side, amongst my treasures." "But the flowers will withet. You will cast them aside to-morrow."

"Such is life. What we cherish to-day "—she just touched the roses upon her breast with caress- ing finger-tips—"passes from us to-morrow. But the diamonds "—with a quick, sweet glance—" will last. So do not despair. You have much to be thankful for."

"You lovely little syren, so I have I And now, may I have a dance? " "As many as you please. But, no. As our en- gagement is not yet announced, you must be con- tent with three. See, my partners are crowding round. So you must not be greedy. Shew your- self generous, and let me enjoy myself to-night." "'You ought to do so more completely with rue than anyone else." "I ought," she murmured under her breath, "but I don't." Then aloud, and smiling sweetly up into his face: You may have two more after supper. The very last on the programme. So cheer up," and, before he had time to reply, she turned to speak to several pleasant-looking 1. young fellows, who were clamouring for dances. The ball was at its height. The dancing-room was hot to suffocation, and towards the end of a waltz, the second they had danced together that night, Verona begged Fred Constantine to take her into the conservatory. Most willingly he complied with her request, and his heart throbbed with a feeling of wild delight, when as they entered and seated themselves in a secluded corner amongst the flowers and branching palms, he looked round and saw that they were alone. Everyone was in the dancing-room or down at supper. This charming retreat, so cool and fragrant, was deserted for the moment, and Verona gave a little sigh of pleasure as she sank into a low seat near a bank of brilliant and sweet-smelling blossoms. "How delicious, Fred, if we could stay here for ever ! "

"Verona! If we could! Here—or anywhere— together." He caught her hand. "My love My love !" His soul was in his eyes. "Oh, mine own, you do love me? You will be my wife?" Verona's face was ghastly. Her lips quivered, and she looked at him in anguish. "Fred, Fred! Why "—she wrung her hands— "oh, why did I not know ? Why not guess? But, alas! I thought only of position—nothing of love; blinded myself to my true feelings ; and my soul is full of woe; for now I suddenly realise what I have lost. But it is too late—too late. I have promised— () Fred ! "—she wrung her hands—"to marry the Earl of Minton."

lie turned upon her in surprise. "To bet. that not very strange ? "

There was a light in his eyes that she did not admire; and, for the first time, she trembled for her beloved Verona's happiness. "She might have been better off after all, if she had loved and married Fred," she thought. "It sounds well to be rich and a Countess, but- However, since it's all settled now, we must just hope for the best." In a storm of weeping and passionate regret for the happiness that she had flung from her, Verona paced her room from end to end. She knew now that she loved Fred Constantine with all her soul; knew that life away from him would be a misery, and that wealth, title, rank, would be nothing to her if in order to secure them she was bound to renounce her love and marry the Earl of Minton. "But I must, I must," she cried, with set teeth

and clenched fists. "I have promised and cannot, without some good reason, break my engagement. And yet if I told him all, threw myself upon his mercy, he might have pity. It is Christmas. A Christmas that I believed would be so happy, a time that ought to be so full of peace, and Oh, I will—l must see him and beg him to set mt free. The misery of it all is too great. My hear* —is—broken."

Next morning, worn out and exhausted, Verona slept long and heavily, and when at last she opened her eyes, Selina stood by her bedside smiling. "It is Christmas Eve, miss," she cried. "The loveliest and brightest we have had for years. You'll never forget it and your wonderful happi- ness. Law It's enough to drive you wild, to think of marrying an Earl, and he that mad about you that-" Verona stopped her with an angry look and a quick wave of her hand, and, surprised, the maid turned away to the dressing-table. Half-an-hour later, white and weary, with dark circles under her beautiful eyes, Verona went' downstairs.

"Lord Minton has been looking for you every- where," Mrs. Fair said, as she met her in the hall. "He's an ardent lover, little woman, and does not like to be neglected. He was quite cross last night because you left the ballroom so early." Verona crimsoned over cheek and brow. "Where is he? I "—her voice trembled—" wish to see him at once." "He is in the card-room. Your father and ho and a select party are playing—gambling I call it, for their stakes are high. So take Lord Minton out. A brilliant Christmas Eve like this should be spent by an impassioned lover, such as he is, on the ice or in the park with his lovely fiande instead of in a card-room. Only shew yourself, my pretty one, and he'll soon forget everything but you." Verona stole into the card-room, and went soft'., across to the table where Lord Minton, her father, and some other men sat together playing. They were absorbed in their game, and not wishing to disturb them, the girl sank into a chair and took up a book.

But she 'could not read. Her thoughts wandered, her mind was ill at ease, her heart sore. She dreaded her interview with Lord Minton, yet longed to get it over. "If he will not reler3e me what shall I do?" she asked herself. And raising her eyes she looked at him closely, examining his features, his expres- sion, the shape of his head, the bend of his shoulders, his hands. "Lord Minton, 1--" The words died upon her lips, and she fell white and senseless to the ground.

Her first thought was of you. You are a lucky fellow." "Oh, you think so, do you ? " he muttered. "Though what the deuce brings women to the card- room beats me. However, she's only a child. I'll soon persuade her she was mistaken," and he strolled into the library. Verona had risen from the sofa and was standing by the fire, her elbows on the mantelpiece, her hand across her eyes. "On Christmas Eve? Impossible! And I will not release you. You must marry me, and "—under his breath—"pay the penalty of your impertinence to-day." "I will never marry you." "They will not believe you." "Assuredly they will. I have never told or acted a lie in my life." "Yet you vowed you loved and would marry me." A crimson wave swept over the lovely face. Christmas Day rose bright and clear, and, break- fast over, Verona threw a fur-lined cloak round her shoulders, and stepping out through the library window wandered off into the frozen woods. Upon every side the Christmas bells were ringing merrily, and the girl stood still, listening with delight to their sweet music, as it was wafted towards her on the breeze.

A NARROW ESCAPE.

Miss Annie Brook, a weaver, living at Eastwood's Buildings, Dark-lane, Mirfield, has had one of the most wonderful escapes it is possible to conceive. So sensa- tional are the facts relating thereto, that to prevent the possibility of disbelief, she has made the following sworn declaration before Mr E B Wilson, Commissioner for Oaths, of Mirfield. This statement fittingly sum- marise.; her case :—" I, Annie Brook, of Dark-lane, Mirfield, in the County of York, weaver, do solemnly and sincerely declare as follows—Four years ago I began to suffer from neuralgia. I had five teeth drawn. 1 was attended by a doctor, but got worse. I suffered from heart disease, ansemia, indigestion, dropsy, and dizziness. No leia; than ten doctors in all attended me, hut I go: DO bettor. I was an in-patient at Dewsbury Infirmary for nine weeks, and an out-patient for a long time. I was at Bridlington for three weeks, but got no benefit. I was mad with pain and could not get relief. I could not go upstairs for nine months, and had to sleep on a sofa. My mother gave me up and prepared my grave- cloths s. For four years my mother had not a whole nights sleep with me. She used to feel at me every few minutes to see if I were dead ; for the doctors said that I might go off at any minute. I began to take Charles Forde's Bile Beans for Biliousness. Eight days after I began I was able to sleep. After taking five boxes I was quite cured again. Nothing but Bile Beans cured me. And I make this solemn declaration, conscien- tiously believing the same to be true, and by virtue of the Statutory Daclarations Act, 1835." Charles Forde's Bile Beans for Biliousness, which brought about the above cure are ob•ainable from all chemists, or from the Bile Bean Manufacturing Co., 119, London Wall, 8.C., by sending prices. is 11d, or 2i 9, for a large box. So much confidence have the proprietors in their prepara- tion that they are willing to send a free sample to every reader who writes to the above address and mentions this advertisement. Needless to say, Miss Brock's story has caused considerable stir in circles where she is well known.

THE CHAIRMAN INDICATES WHERE REFORM IS REQUIRED.

Mr. J. Moseley Williams (the chairman) pointed to the great hardships to which workingmen's families were subject through being complelkd to provide medical certificates.at their own cost, as evidence of the inability ot their children to attond school, and cited a case particular to Frodsbam in which a poor woman was un- able to pay toe 61 per c,rtificate which the medical officer demandei. He strzgested that some course should be tat en which would provide for the payment of such certificates out of the-public funds. He moved no resolution, but simply brought the matter forward in the hope the members present would look into it. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Linaker agreed, and thought there should be some provision whereby the wives ot workmen should have the necessary certificates at the public mit. He thought that the public should pay where the child was really ill, but that the parents themselves should bear the coet of medical examination in eases which were not genuine. Fire .in Collin-street Warrington.

Shortly alter 2.30 on Sunday atte—rnoon a fire broke out on the premises of Mr. Llewellyn, green- grocer, Collin-street. It originated in one of the bedrooms, though how, it is not known. Informa- tion of the outbreak was sent to the fire station, and the brigade promptly turned out. On their arrival at the scene of the fire it was found that their services were not required, as the flames had been extinguished by the neighbours. Considerable damage was done, most of the furniture in the bed- room being destroyed.

CHRISTMAS.

This festival seems to have been unknown in the early and pure days of the Church of Christ. It was only in the fourth century that it began to be observed at Rome whence it spread to the East. And Neander suggests that it may have been de- signed to draw away the Christian people from the Pagan festival of the shortest day, the birthday of the Sun about to return once more towards the earth. But whatever its origin may have been, we in this country lovingly cling to its observance, and hail its annual occurrence with varied, but in all cases, pleasurable emotions. To most of us it brings dear memories of early and happier years when as children we loved to look up to its bright morning stars, and think of the mysterious baba who was born so long ago in a cave at Bethlehem. It is also the time above all others when friends meet to ex change kindly greetings and to talk of life's experi- ences and of the oeloved ones who have passed on before us to the "other side." Nor can we dwell upon its sacred incidents without being reminded of another and future coming of the Lord. When that promised time comes the kings and their armies who now fill the world with violence and blood shall be swept away forever, and He who was once the child of Bethlehem shall roign in peace and righteousness over a redeemed and righteous people. Ye stars that gem night's dusky brow, And cheer the gloom below ; 0 shine with brighter lustre now, Still more your glory show : For He who placed your orbs on high, In silence there to shine : Now comes to lighten every eye With beams of love divine. * Ye winds and waves that loudly roar In conflict o'er the deep: Break gently on the lonely shore, As round the cliffs ye sweep: For Jesus comes in mercy mild To bid earth's conflicts cease ; And la) its storms and passions wild With everlasting peace. 0 Earth, on David's sacred hill his lowly cave prepare: And with the evening dews distil Your purest fragrance there : For when He comes, no more a guest, But King eternal crowned: His glory on the world shall rest, And make it holy ground. And 0 let men break forth in praise ; How could they silent be! He comes through death from death to raise To immortality!

J. H. W. CLINDINNING.

NOTES ON UAM ES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. [By " Ovalist."]

There is a deal of difference in class between Warrington • and Sa'fore though their performances during the season are pretty much on a par and eacn has beaten the other. The Reds took their revenge on Saturday last when the strongest team Warrington c@uld put in the field was beaten by thirteen points to nil, the victors at the same time being minus both their regular centres, Varty and T Williams. Substitutes almost equally efficient were found and the class of the team was not lowered to any appreciable degree. It is class which Warrington so badly need, that unmistakable but intangible something which wins matches and raises a team out of the common ruck. Salford often do badly, but they still possess class, and to say that it is sure to tell is a stock phrase but none the less true. On Saturday Warrington had, think, rather more of the game than the Reds, but they lacked the scoring power and the ability to make the most of chances. It was here where Salford shone. In the first half they defended to a very large extent but every occasional visit to the ‘Varrington end brought paints. There was no lack of energy, and ability of a kind, on the losing side, but the lack of I finish spelt defeat from the start and will in nine cases out of ten as long as the game is played.

It never struck me before that there was any special appropriateness about the name of the Salford ground. but there is. Of " New Barns " " there are a few." For instance, a couple of them do duty for accommodat- inz the Press, and others add either to the comfort or discomfort of the crowd. Much depends on the weather, as usual. The " barns" in which the Press are bonded would be the airiest places imaginable on a torrid summer day, but on days like Saturday when a 4•7 inch southeaster is having its fling they are the reverse of pleasant habitations. You are well up aloft, and the gale wreaks first vengeance on the elevated pencil- pushers. There is no escape. Open shutters let in all the ozone there is room for, and at the same time play a'Abang-crasla crescendo accompaniment. Numerous cracks and spaces hi tr, roof and sides of the " barns " introduce a pleasing variety of cross-currents and shrill whistlings. They're stimulating places,and consumptiveS desirous of giving the open-air cure a full, fair trial can- not do better than rent this portion of New Barns as a winter residence. It will either kill or cure, and in either eventuality the patient parts with the consump- tion.

There is only one other thing equal to this in the way of specialities at New Barns, and that is the road lead— ing to the ground. I have previously spoken in praise of this thoroughfare with its luxurious carpet of yield- ing mud. They have concentrated the sludge since I was last there by making a totally inadequate footpath down the racecourse side. To keep on this you have to shove s‘mebony eks off. For once in a way the weak- est do not "go Co tha wall."

The game was not at all a bad one. Generally the ball was kept loose and open play on both sides was common. Salford naturally played the threequarter game, and played it well, thc,ugh the half backs did the bulk of the scoring. It is to be counted against the watchfulness of Burges3 and Duckworth that their vis- a-vis, Griffiths and Grey both notched a try in the same manner and in tho same place. The Welsh pair were very smart and played as well as ever they did • Salford would have dene much better in the past if they had stuck consistently to these two and not tried so many "mixtures." There was a noticeable difference in the passing of the two teams. Salford travelled down the field, and Warrington went straißtit across. Oae method resulted in tries and tbo other ended in touch. This is wlsere the art of c•:ntre play comes in and class begins to tell. Oace a centre gets through and the wing, if any good, is a certain scorer.

It is a pity Warrington did not follom up thoir suc- cess in capturing Burgess, by securinz his club-mate, Bone. They hesitated to) long as usual and said Bone was only a sprinter. Perhaps he was, but he is a foot— baller now and a most dangerous wing. Tall, well built, and trernendcusly fast, be is sure of a rich harvest of tries in the future if he has the luck to escape injury. At Salford they think a lot of him.

The best of the Warrington backs was widoubtedly Jack Hallam who played a magnificent game all through under conditions the reverse ot pleasant. A high wind and greasy ground are worse, perhaps, for the full back than any member of the team, but they seemed to have no effect on the Warrington custodian's display, which was a huge surprise to the home crowd. The latter have got into the habit of thinking that Dan Smith is the one and only full back in Lancashire. On. Saturday they saw their own favourite excelled. Isherwood was also in good form and made several dashing runs which ought to have been more profit able than they were. Fish was not quite so good as usual and lost a certain try by dodging too much. Laval( ss and Hai ris were both smart at times, but on the attack chey were outcla,sed. The same remark applies to the halves,whose only merit was 'the vantity of hard work they put in. Forward War- rington did fairly well and got possession often enough, but the bigger men of Salford played a superior game, Bucl,llr and Bhapps being particularly active and useful. Phillips made a good centre and looked well after Bone, while the halves were very resourceful.

The Salford match was a very critical one for the winners, for had they been vanquished their chance of making any more progress in the league wouA have btan almost extinguished. Warrington were in a similar position and have got as hikh as they are likely to do this season. Wigan furnished the surprise of the day by defeating Leigh, who have fallen off a lot lately.

Warrington will have their hands full this week-end, for Oldham, St. Heleas;and Runcorn all pay Wildergpool a visit. St. Helens are met for the third time, and War- rington should have no difficulty in entering the third and final round for the West Lancashire Cup. The two League matches are two of the best, from the spectators' point of view, of the whole season. Can Warrington win thorn ? We know well enough that it will be easy to lose them, but can the team just for once rise to the occasion and smite these clubs. Oldham have lost a few times in recent years at Warrington, and lost the charnpion3hip here last season, while Runcorn invar- iably win. The odds are against the home club, but if they play with that grim determination Which occasion- ally characterises them they might have a very joyous Christmas at the expense of three clUbs they wild dearly love to vanquirh.

Angling. A new association has blossomed out under the name of the " The Warrington Workingmen's Angling ASBC- ciation." It offers intending members cheap travelling, free fishing rights in the Shropshire Union Canal, be- tween Ellesmere Port, Audlem, Wardle Lock and Hhos— wiel and the Lancaster Canal. A match to be flAhed for, prizes to the value of nearly £20,a1l for is entrance and 41 per month after. An opening match has been arranged to take place on May 25th for priz.s offered by the President of one gold, ore gold-centre and one silver medal. There will be other prizes also, including one for the largest specimen fish oaught in the match. This opening match in no way interferes with the annual match. The head quarters of the Association are the General Wolfe, the Gladston e Arms being a branch. Intending members can join at either place. The first night for receiving contributions is on January Bth. The bon secretary is Mr. G. Hamblea, 5, Egerton-stre3t, Howley, Warring Lon.

NORTHERN UNION. LANCASHIRE SENIOR COMPETITION.

Salford Defeat Warrington.

Warrington pail their return visit to Now Barns, Sal- ford, on Saturday and rocoived the customary thrash- ing. The thy was rough and cold, and the ground in very heavy order, but about 6,000 speetatore assembled to witness what proved a well coirested game. Salford were without T. Williams and Varty, both injurod, Phillips going centre, Dan Smith wing. and Hignett full back. Warrington turned out as selected, Mr. Kidd, (Wilm) was the referee, and the teams linel out as follows : Warrington : Hallam, full back ; Fish, Isherwood Lawless, and Harris, three-quarters ; Burgeas and Duck- worth, half hacks ; Taylor. Boardman, Eden, Mere- weather, Saholtza, J Swift, F Swift, and Fairhurst, for- wards. Salford : Flignett, full hack ; Bone, Phillips, Hadwen and D Smith, three-quarters; Grey and Griffiths, half backs; Rhappa, J Williams, Suaw, Tunney, Buckler, Heath, Worthington, and Fisher, forwards. Taylor started for Warrington against the wind, Rhappa quickly leading the Reds to the attack. 'Griffiths, Phillips. and Bone handled smartly from the first scrimmage, the wing man being upset by Fish near the line. Salford were smart on the attack, and getting possession onco more Gre7 scored an easy try at the corner, Phillips failing to augment. By short punting and speedy forward play Warrington drove Salford to the far-end. Mereweather picked up in the open and made for the line but was grassed when becoming dangerous. Fish had a penalty shot at goal, but the wet ball failed to rise. Warrington got on the line as the result of a mull by Bone and there was a sharp struggle for some minutes. Another penalty against the Reds preyed to their advantage however, and Warring- ton fell back a little. They still attacked energetically. A fine passing bout traversed the length of the Salford goal-line, Harris missing the final transfer with the line at his mercy. Salford broke away, and Phillips giving Isherwood the go-by passed to Bone who had a clear run in from near the centre and scored in good position. Phillips landed a goal. Warrington rushed to the other end again Fish making a dash but falling a victim to numbers. Bone got clear again and made for the line at a great speed, Hallam just getting across in time to stop the flyer. Salford were rewarded very soon after, Phillips kicking another goal from a penalty. More dribbling by Warrington gave them the upper hand and quick transfers gave Harris possession near the line, but Dan Smith was up and bowled him over very promptly. Hallam played a great game and saved his side on innumerable ocoahions, the Salford crowd cheering hi a efforts repeatedly. Another attack from the home team was fruitful, Griffiths bluffing the Warrinaen halves and repeating his partner's success. Phillip missed the goal. Fish then picked up tram the loo;e and made a splendid run on the heavy turf, but lost his chance by dodging to- wards the centre. He could easily have scored at the corner. Half-time came with Salford leading by 13 points to nil. Heath reaumed the game, Dan Smith having gone to his proper position, and flignett playing wing. The Reds were first to attack, Griffiths having a drop at goal which went wide. Warrington cleared the ievadere out and took up a promising position. Hadwen smartly intercepted and ran half the length of the field, pas- leg forward forward to Hignett at 'the finish. Smith essayed another drop, Fairhurst and Scholtze responding with a long dribble down the field. At this stage Lawiess went off with an injured shoulder, Eden playing on the wing and Fish going centre. Salford had hardly begun to make their superior numbers felt when Griffiths strained his ankle and had to retire, Phillips filling the vacancy and Heath operating at centre. The teams were now on even terms again. except in regard to points. War- rington were principally attacking, Fish doing smart vzoik. He looked like running through once when Burgess gave him a miss and passed over his head. Had- wen and Smith defended well, but Warrington should have scored from the numerous openings which pre- sented themselves. Fish missed another gmal-kick from Isherwood's mark, and the end came with the score reading, Salford 2 goals, 3 tries, (1.3 points) ; Warrington nil.

Runcorn Defeat Stockport.

Although there is undoubtedly great local rivalry between these teams, their meeting at Runcorn en Satur- day last, attracted no larger a crowd than 2,000. Run corn were still minus J Butterworth, and Faulkner, but the visitors turned up with lull strength. Here are the names : Runcorn: S Houghton, back; D Jones, T Warder, H Jones and Wilkinson, three-qnerters ; Jolley and Richardson, half-hacks ; Lightfoot, Farmer, Darlingtun, Gayter, Hughes. Tomlinson, Reece and Walker,forwards. Stockport: Chesters, back ; Robinson, Woreley, Bevan, and Hodgson, three-quarters; Booth and Williams. half-backs ; Trotter, Oram, Garside, Griffiths, Taylor, Burrows. Palmer and Simister, forwards. Favoured by the toss Runcorn put the visitors to face sun and a strong breeze blowing directly down the field. Garside commenced and warder replied so effectively that scrimmaging in the Stockport half became the order. Rune.orn were pen-lised but the free kick re- lieved Stockport very little. Jolley punted well, and Cheaters was grassed on his own line. Things looked gloomy but Booth effectively relieved. Jolley then marked, and had a shot for the uprights, without any accruing benefit. Continuing to have all the argument Farmer and Jones tried breaking through, but it was not until "Bertie," the new man, secured the ball that the first score was recorded. Jolley made a much better attempt at goal and struck the posts. From the kick. out Stockport reached Runcorn quarter flag but Hough- ton was in a happy mood, and compelled a retreat. Farmer claimed fora dropped goal, and did not get it. Then Bevan fed by Booth set off with some capable passing which the Runcorn forwards gleefully broke up. Houghton then came into prominence for a wretched shot at goal, but a moment later redeemed himself by stopping a Stockport rush. Following up Houghton ran well and parted to Warder who was carried into touch only just in time to save a score. Run- corn secured many splendid openings through off- side play on the part of the Stockport forwards but the wind carried the ball too far for them to effec- tively attack, and allowed Cheiters and Worsley, who Played capitally for the visitors, plenty of time to clear. Richardson and Jones were responsible for a run that , caused a touch-clown to be conceded. Booth cleared from a scrum, and Houghton receiving replied. Bevan made effort after effort. but Warder nullified hi; at- tempts. The Runcorn forwards got away Walker scor- ing what appeared to be a good try, but the point wag disallowed. Richardson, Jolley, Warder and Jones tried several times to score.but their movements were lacking cohesion, and Stockport saved repeatedly, though dead balls were common. The game became anything but "class." Too much roughnese was evident, and things were unintereeting until Jolley set up a farther attack, and the latter added a penalty goal. Developing into roughness again it became necessary for the referee tc caution players on both sides. Continuing to press up to the interval Runcorn could not further improve their score. : Runcorn, i 2 goals, 1 try ( 7

points) ; Stockport, nil. Even against the wind Runcorn proved much the better combination. Wilkinson soon established a strong position on the visitors' right,and kicking across enabled Walker to score. Jolley added the goal. The visitors were evidently surprised, and it vas some time before they could get their feet. Whilst they were in this condition Booth passed to Bevan, at least he tried to, but Wilkin- son snapping it up sailed beautifully a way and registered what to Stockport was an astonishing try. Jolley again steered the oval through the posts. From this point Stockport seemed to realize that something was required of them, and for the first time became really dangerous. Houghton failing to catdh a touch- kick, Warder preveated a score by the narrowest of shaves. Runcorn were several times closely hemmed in, but the defence was cleaner and far more effective than bad Stockport's been when " the!boot was on the other leg" in the first half. Indeed, Cheaters was visited many times; and Jolley missed an almost certain try by pass- ing to Jones, who failed to take it, although Jolley had simply parted to make doubly sure. Later Stockport backs again combined well, but the prettiest piece of passing came from the Runcorn forwards a few minutes later. Hodgson dropped a goal, but no further in-road was made. Final result :—Runcorn, 4 goals, 3 tries, 17 points; Stockport, 1 goal, 2 points.

Yorkshire v. Cumberland.

This match in the Northern Union's County tourna- ment was played at Hull.
