Warrington Defeated by-Salford.

Warrington suffered defeat at the hands of Salford on Saturday. The match took via ce on the Wilderspoo ground before about 3,000 spectators. During the first half the home team had the advantage of the wind, and at the outset attacked, but injudicious kicking was taken advantage of by Salford. Fine back play by Salford was chellred by Knight, but another outcome of pissing saw Harter score at the corner, no goal resulting. Warrington attacked, Hockenhall being pulled up near the posts. From sharp play Garrity equalised, Salford played up briskly and scored again just before the inter- val. Half-time :Salford, two tries ; Warrington, one try. Playing with the wind, Salford maintained the aggres- sive. Passing movements were checked by the resolute tackling of Hockenhall and Ratcliffe. Smith being pulled up at the corner after a fine run. Owen was next hell up on the line. Salford negatived a forward rush by Hughes and Allen. and Owen was again held up. Drops at goal went wide. Salford scorei on time. Final : Salford, three tries (nine points) ; Warrington, one try (three points).

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL. WARRINGTON CUP. Second Round

Warrington St. Mary's v. St. Helens Recs.

At Howley, on Saturday. The opening exchange; were greatly in favour of the visitors. Osborne having all his work cut out to keep his charge clear. J Mather relieved with a huge kick. Albert Meachin made a fins run, but the force of his shot was stayed in the goal mouth. The Combination men again pressed, and after some exciting play in goal, White scored for St. Helens. The visiting forwards again got going, but Henneghan and J Mather both proved stumbling-blocks. Good work by 'Marley and Robinson got play in the visitors' quarters. After a spell of uninteresting play. the St. Helens men broke away, a long shot by Tabbern just dropping over the bar: Half-time : St. Helens Racs, one goal ; Warrington St• Mary's. nil. On resuming,the homesters pressed, T Mather !laving very hard lines. The Rees got the ball away. but Marley returned, and Meachin getting possession, passed to T Mather, that player equalizing. Tabbern immediately after gave the visitors the lead. The homesters still kept up a spirited attack in which the Mather trio were very conspicuou.. Albert Meachin had hard luck with a shot that struck the croas•bar. Final : St. Helens Recs, two goals ; Warrington St. Mary'e, one goal:

Coming of Age Celebration.

Between 50 and 60 60 of the Engineering Depart- ment of Messrs. N Greeninlz and Sons Limited, sat down to dinner last .N riday night in the Midland Hotel, to celebrate the coming of age of Mr. Joseph Waring. The dinner was carried out in first class style by Mr and Mrs Wakefield. A lengthy pro- gramme followed, the foreman Mr. C F Taylor in the chair. Toasts : "The Queen "by the Chairman "Army, Navy. and Volunteers" by Mr. P O'Brien; "Town and trade of Warrington." by Mr. W Payne; toast of the evening by Mr E S Wood. Mr. Waring suitably replied. Songs were rendered by Messrs. Elliott, Millington, Travis, Armstrong, Taylor. Turton and Hay,and a recitation by Mr W Williams The accompanist was Mr W Pemberton. Votes of thanks were tendered to the Chairman, and the committee for carrying out the arrangements, everyone feeling highly delighted and satisfied that a pleasant evening had been spent.

Re Burial Fees.

Sir,—Allow me to say how delighted I was with the outspoken utterances of Alderman J. Shaw-Green arid a few more at the Council meeting last week. lam glad we are not all tainted with cle:icalism. The working- man wants his money as well as the " over-fed " cleric, and is ofttimes raore worthy of it. Councillor Scnethurst Ehouts about his 8.1. friends having to thrive on £BO per annum, or three-eights more than the average workingtnan gets. But Councillor Smethurst need not feel alarmed, his friends look very well, notwithstand- ing. Parsons now-a-days have one great lesson to learn, and that is : to be like their Master, " Sell what ye have and give to the prior." Dint rob thew, for the" poor ye have always with you."—Yours, Sze., HENRY ROBIN.

Garston Place, Sankey Bridges. - Christmas Cheer for Ironworkers.

Sir—lt has been suggested that my letter of last week has conveyed a rang impregsion with regard to the Christmas cheer to bekiven to the unemployed Iron workers of Warrington. What the Committee and I as secretary are trying is to provide a good dinner. groceries, 8-z,L, for the wives and little ones in the maoy distressed homes. Our in- tention is not to give anything to the regular men who may be working to or three days a week, but to the bye-turn men and mill men who are out of employment through no fault of their own hut purely on account of the slackness in the Iron trade.

In no CAS. will money be g!ven but tickets to the value (according to the size of each family), on different tradesmen in the town, to the full amount the Com- mittee realize through the subscription lists now before the public. Donations in money or food will be thankfully re- ceived and acknowledged in the locll papers of Saturday the 29th inst.

Apolozising for tremnassing upon your valuable space I remain yours faithfully. T. B. MALKIN.

2, Lilford-street, Warrinenn

Corporation Contracts and Accounts.

Sir.—What I and many other ratepayers in Warring- ton consider objectionable in orders and contracts given out by our Corporathn is the very unfair and improper preference given to certain goods and materi Is which can only be supplied by certain firms,the agent or person who has the sole right of supplying the same in this district being contie:itcd with our Town Council. If this applied to some p ttent or speciality which could not be supplied by any other firm that would tif=, a proper excuse. Take the Parr Hall committee as only one case (there are many others). That committee when letting the Hall to any private person puts this clause in the agreement.

" No. 18—Arrangements have been made with the Warrington and District Billpesting and Advertisirm Company, Limited, of Bnwvy Chambers, Warrington for the posting on their stations at their usual rates of all bills, placards, notices issued or used in connection with all engagements of the Dail and any hirer or hirers advertising by means of bills, placards, notices!, &1., in the borough and district must advertise with the above company exclusively."

There are many clauses besides this but strange to say this is the only one that is underlined. On seeing this clause made so strict and knowing that we had two Bill Postinz Companies in Warrington I got a list of the shareholders of the company which the Parr Hall Committee had taken under its wing and I was not at all surprised to see the names of town councillors among its shareholders. Many ratepayers think it is time' to stop this sort of improper way of conducting Corporation business' There is a clique in our Town Council whose members are connected with limited companies and they are doing all they can to keep matters dark and at the same time discourage anything that I and others try to bring forward. This clique is busy circulating a report that I am bringing forward these improper tradings of interested parties because I am interested in a brick- works and want to get Corporation contracts myself hut the argument is all nonsense at the present time because the Corporation surely are not going to build another Police Station, Electric Light Station or Infestions Disease Hospital nor are the Guardians of the Poor thinking of building another Workhouse Hospital. There is however the new Sanatorium but as the contract is let and the bricks specified (which are of course not Warrington bricks) there is an end of that matter. I knew when I :ook this matter up that everything this clique could say would be said and not lose anything in the telling. I therefore purposely waited till these jobs were well advanced so that this argument would not apply, but they have got many unthinking people to believe there is something in it. It is, how- ever, simply the old came of drawing the red herring across the trail. We can, I think, ask our Cor- poration to do what the Government is willing to do. Last week a department in placing orders for machinery with several firms, specified that a certain part of the machine should be of a certain make which could only be obtained from one firm and on its being pointed out to the Government that the several firms themselves made these parts and it was very obnoxious for these firms who could make them to go to this one favoured firm, that part of the specification was, to the great satis- faction of these firms, withdrawn by the Government. Ratepayers are asking me many times a day if there is anything being done by the Corporation in these matters. I can oily say that up to the present I have not had any reply whatever but no doubt the Corpora- tion have the matters under consideration and if I receive any communication I shall with your permis- sion publish it in this paper.—Yours truly,

1 on-licensed public-house or beer house to every 750 of the {population in towns, and 1 to every 400 in country districts. A term of, say, 7 years should be adopted as the basis of a time notice, and com- pensation arrangement. The amount necessary for the money compensation should be raised by an annual license rental levied on the rateable value of licensed premises. The charging of such a rental is both necessary for the compensation scheme, and just in itself; for the present license duties are extremely low, and ought to be raised apart from any other consideration as may be clearly seen from the high prices fetched by the sale of licensed houses; in the case of any new licenses being granted, an even higher license rental might be charged. These license rentals would not consti- tute any claim to continuance of a license, for they would be paid, as are the present license duties, in return for certain privileges lasting one year and no longer. As the rateable value is the basis of the license , rental, so it is to be the basis of the amount of compensation. The licensing authority should have discretion to make the reduction all at once at the beginning of the seven years, or to spread it by withdrawing one seventh of the surplus licenses each year. The compensation paid for the licenses withdrawn at the beginning of the first year would be an amount not exceeding seven times the ratE- able value of the premises. For those withdrawn the second year the compensation would be six times the rateable value, and so on. Any holder of a license might be given the option of taking his compensation and dropping his license at the beginning of the period, and this would give an o;aportunity to the owners of licensed property to arrange among themselves very largely the required reduction. The money required to be pain at once could be realised by a loan on the security of the lice ;Ise rentals.

The money compensation should be distributed am-mg all the parties interested in the premises as enhanced in value by the license, and if they could not, agree among themselves, as to the proportion of their interests,they would have to go to the expense of applying to an arbitrator.

_ In 'comparing the amounts to be paid in compen- sation with the much larger amounts now paid for the licensed premises themselves, it must be re- membered, (I) that the premises themselves, often of considerable value for other purposes, corner sites and so on, would remain in the possession of their owners; (2) that in many cases the owner of the licensed house owns also the adjoining property, which would be enhanced in value, for no one likes to live next door to a public-house; (3) that under the tied-house systlm the loss will be spread, and brewing companies having an interest in licenses abolished, will partially recoup themselvei by ham aged trade elsewhere; indeed, if their own contention were accepted, almost wholly. At the end of the 7 years the license rentals should continue to be paid into the Imperial Exchequer to be used for whatever purposes Parlia- ment may direct; they would no longer be required for compensation; because all compensation claims would be definitely at an end, and it would be clearly understood that they could not revive. A careful study of the whole subject of c )mpen- don leads irresistibly to the conclusion that some such scheme must be adopted, or none at all. Though a great deal of sentiment has been importei into the matter, it is after all a purely practical one. If you are going to give more compensation, where is it to come from? Once for all it is clear that the public will not stand being taxed for this purpose. If you levy a greater amount on the houses that remain they will not be able to stand the tax. It can only be done by lengthening the time limit indefinitely, and making the compensation scheme continuous in its action; this is nothing but confirming a per- petual vested Interest in licente-holders and p1.,- Renting a constant. obstacle to further reforms. It is better to have no scheme of compensation and reduction at all. But the necessities of the case cry urgently for a large reduction, and no one who has studied the question can be deaf to that appeal.

THE ST. HELENS EXPLOSION

CASE.

Verdict for the Corporation.

Mr. Justice Bucknill and the Government. The action brought by the Bt. Helens Corporation against the United Alkali Company came again before Mr. Justice Bucknill in London on Wednesday. The Corporation seek to recover"compensation for the damage done by the terrible explosion which occurred at thn Company's works last year, and which killed five people and wrecked a quantity of property. The evidence was heard at th 3 rcc Int Liverpool Assizes, an I the jury gave a verdict in the form of answer to a series of questions. In effect they found tha', it was not known to the defendants before the accident that unmixed chlorate of potash was an explosive, but that in the manufacture and storage of the material they did not take all reasonable care and precaution to prevent accident by fire or explosion, and that the fire on which t' e explosion followed was not one which happened by mere chance and without rpgligence The effect of the verdict was argued on ‘Vednesday bsfore his Lardship. The counsel foi the defendants maintained that the jury's finling of want of knowledge of the explosive charterer of the chemical concerned released the Company from liability. Without calling on the counsel for the Corpora- tion Mr. Justice Bucknill gave lodgment in favour tot the plaintiffs, with costs, expressing his concurrence in the jury's finding of want of reasonable care and pre- caution. The amount of the damages is to be assessed privately. StAv of execution, however, was granted in view of an appeal. Other cases.it is understool,dlynd on the result of the Corporation's action. In the course of his judgment Mr. Justice Bucknill remarked that he was astonished that the Government, with this terrible explosion before their eyes, had not made any rules es to the storage of the chemical which had caused it.

L RIGHIS RIME:VIM. j LO NSING REFORM,

BY THE HON. SIDNEY PEEL.

vii. The Cornoensation Question.

So far we have been dealing with amendments in the licensing law, which might be expected to meet with a very general assent, except from those directly interested in the liquor trade. We have now to deal with the question of compensaion, which nor only stands in the way of that large re- duction of licensed houses, which is, as we have shewn, so urgently necessary, but also presents an effectual obstacle to the adoption of any more far reaching reform, such as local permissive prohibi- tion or some adaptation of the principles of the Gothenburg system, which will some day have to be tried, unless a very striking improvement takes place. It cannot be too emphatically asserted that there is no legal claim whatever to compensation for the loss of a public-house. The law is clear upon the point that a license lasts for one year and no longer; the licensing authority are under no com- pulsion to renew it More than that, a license is granted for a year to a particular man for a par- ticular house, but there is by law no property in a license. It cannot be bequeathed. Even when a man dies or becomes bankrupt during the currency of the license, it does not vest in his legal repre- sentatives or the trustee in bankruptcy, but they have to make a formal application for its transfer,

It is true that this lias- not been always well known in the past, a great many people having laboured under the delusion that the law was other- wise, but since the case of Sharp v. Wakefield, de- cided in 1892, the truth has been made abundantly plain to all parties concerned. No amount of argu- ing from analogy, and no small differences between the procedure in applying for a license for new premises, and that on application for premises which have been previously licensed, can have the smallest weight against the express declarations of the law.

It is sometimes said that, because the licensing laws recognise the owner of licensed premises as distinct from the holder of the license, and in cer- tain cases give him a right of application for a transfer of a license, the perpetuity of the license is thereby implied. But it is a strange interpreta- tion of the law to construe an applica ion for a license as a right to obtain it, and moreover even the right of application only endures during the currency of the license, which lasts for a yeat and no longer It would be strange too, if the law re- cognised the perpetuity of !he license in the owner of the premises who has not got it, but not in the occupier,to whom it is ac ually granted, and whose character and circumstances are inquired into to see whether he is a fit and proper person to hold it, while those of the owner are not. Yet that is the absurdity which the brewer argument lays down. For the brewer certainly recognises no right to compensation in his yearly tenant; in his case the terms of the agreement are strictly construed,and he is not on any account allowed to put forward the plea that there is any continuing interest in the license which h. 93 been granted to him. The state of the law is e complete answer to those claims for money compensation out of public funds put forward by some licensed traders, esti- mated apparently on the basis that the license is ! granted in perpetuity. _ _ _

The quesfion" ma i be regarded from another point of view. The justices could, if they chose, enormously diminish the value of any licensed house by licensing the house next door, and the policy of free licensing was at one time deliberately applied for a period of four or five years in Liver- pbol, as well es for a longer period in a neighbour- ing county division But no one would argue that the existing license-holders were entitled to com- pensation for the loss which they would sustain; nor, when the legislature has from time to time placed restrictions upon the hours of opening, has it ever been supposed that the publican or the brewer ought to be compensated for the loss thus sustained; and yet the cut big off of two or three hours of sale in one house might probably mean a greater loss to the brewer, than if two or three licenses in an over-crowded district were altogether withdrawn. CIRCUMSTANCES TO TAKE INTO ACCOUNT.

But while from the point of view of strict justice the claim to compensation cannot be for a moment admitted, there are circumstances which make it advisable to give some amount of compensation. (the word compensation is perhaps a bad one to use, for it might imply some kind of claim; some neutral term like consideration would be bet er, but compensation has come to be used in the popu- lar sense of an allowance for the suppression of a license).

Until recently it was very commonly believed that there was a vested interest in a license, and a great deal of money has been invested often by small in- vestors on the security of licensed property in the belief that this was so. Although a certain number of licenses are withdrawn every year for no other reason than that they ar.i not required, this belief has been largely fostered by the action of the jus- tices in renewing licenses year after year when they could not possibly be thought to be required, and a wholesale reduction, such as alone would effec-, a great and permanent benefit, would undoubtedly be a chanee of policy on the part of the licensing au- thority, which past experience has not taught people to expect it is both reasonable and politic to effect a change, which would touch so many per- sons, in a manner as little abrupt and as well calcu- lated to ease the financial strain on individuals as possible, without unduly sacrificing the national interest

Again it must be pointed out that compensation in the case of a reduction of licenses stands on a different footing from compensation in the case of complete suppression of licenses in a whole district' In the latter case the licenses would all be taken away, because in the opinion of the inhabitants the liquor trade as a whole was an unqualified nuisance, and deserved complete extirpation, whereas, when the number is merely reduced, the assumption is that it is the excessive number of licenses, which is an intolerable nuisance, and not the whole trade; if no allowance were made in some form or other for the suppressed licenses, a distinct inequality would be created; some licensed houses would have the benefit of remaining to reap the advantage of diminished competition, when they could not be said to have deserved it by any superiority of con- duct over their rivals.

This distinction points to the solution of the difficulty, awl suggests, what is now regarded as a settled principle by all parties, that all money com- pensation should come from the licensed houses that remain. Another expedient, which may be fairly called in to assist in easing the strain of a change, is to make some allowance of time, that is to substitute security of tenure on good behaviour during a given period of years instead of the pre- sent annual tenure. Compensation by a time limit has, however a difficulty to face; if a particular license holder knows that he will certainly lose his lice se at the end of a given number of years. he is likely to strain every nerve to make the bes-, of his opportunities, and a good deal of mischief is likely to arise, which even the best administration may find it very difficult to cope with.

===== 0002601_19001221_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
IF COMPENSATION IS GIVEN,

Assuming then that compensation may fairly be given, partly by a money payment to he raised by contributions levied on the houses that remain, and partly by a time limit, certain considerations may be laid down as essential to the formulation of any scheme. First the contributions by the houses that remain must not be levied in such a manner as to confer upon these houses any claim to a vested in- terest. It is most important not to pledge the future more than can possibly be helped; we cannot tell what view may be taken on these questions by succeeding generations, and no additional obstacles must be thrown in the way of any reforms which they may vv ish to carry out. The compensation scheme must be regarded as a temporary expedient to meet a removable evil, and must come to an end within a definite time. Secondly, the time limit must be so arranged as tameet the danger we have referred to. Thirdly, the scheme must not be of such a character as to injure the administration of the law during s continuance That is to say, the licensing authority must not be deprived of the power to refuse a license for any reason that seems good to them, which is the founctation of their power in administration; they must have the power to refuse compensation if good cause is shown.

Such a scheme as that proposed in the Minority 'Report of the Royal Commission fulfils these condi- tions ; espo-i!Llly it provides that at the end of a given period all claim to compensation is to cease absolutely,and the field is to be leftelear for further reforms, if these are found to be necessary and desirable by public opinion. This scheme is in outline as follows. It is pro- posed that a statutory maximum should be fixed of

===== 0002601_19001221_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
MkJORITY REPORT SCFIEME CRITICISED

The difficulties of a scheme of compensation are well illustrated by a scheme put forward in the lqajority Report of the Royal Commission. It pro- poses to compensate for all suppressed licenses by giving the full value as declared by the owners and occupiers of the licensed premises. The compensa- sat im fund is to be raised by a duty of 64 81 per cell% on the same declared value, with contribu- tions from clubs and hotels, and higher duties charged on new licenses. At the beginning of each septennial period a sum is to be raised by loan equal to that which would be realised from these sources during that period,and as many licenses suppressed as the sum thus obtained will avail to buy out. Un- less Parliament intervenes at the end of the seven years,the same process is to be repeated for another seven years and so on.

This-scheme, to which are appended the signa- tures of all the Commissioners representing the liquor trade, accepts the principle equally with the Minority scheme that money compensation can only come from the houses that remain. Thil is an im- portant point, but a scrutiny of the scheme reveals some very significant features. First it assumes that all license holders are entitled to full com- pensation on the basis of the value declared by them; in other words it seeks to establish a com- plete vested interest in the license. This vested in- terest is to continue from one septennial period to another, unless Parliament intervenes at the be- ginning of any septennial period. If there was at present a legal vested interest Parliament would have the power at any moment to intervene and put an end to it so that the license holder under the scheme would be in a stronger position than he would be now, if a legal vested interest in the license were established un- conditionally. When the proposal is compared with the actual state of the law, it will at once he seen what an immense advantage it seeks to confer.upon the license holder, what an impediment to effective administration by the licensing authority and what a cmitinual obstacle to future reform. Snowily something might possibly be said for the establish- ment of the vested interest, if any real solid good Was to be gained. The scheme sets out from the proposition that a large and immediate reduction of licenses is necessary, but what is the effect of the scheme itself ? On a liberal calculation the reduc- tion of licenses in each septennial period would be about 3 or at the very outside 4 per cent. of the whole number, which is a good deal less than the reduction which usually takes place under the operation of the present law. And in return for this miserable result. Perlin- mends to be asked to confer a vested interest upon license holders, and thus firmly establish and fortify by law the position of the liquor trade al- ready strong enough in fact, though not in law and theory._

It Is impossible to escape from the difficulty, that if you give what is called full compensation on any bakii like that of present market value, you cannot get any reduction worth having, much less clear the way for future reforms. Either you must have a scheme of satisfactory reduction coupled with rompensation on a basis a great deal below the present market valuei.or you must have no compen- sation schema at all. And the longer a scheme of reasonable compensation is delayed, .the more liksly drel it become that one day a scheme of reduction will be enforced without any compensation at all ; for time is always running on without any Act of Parliament, and the day may come when the nation will decide that the tirre limit thus allo wed hos been sufficient compensation.

Sudden Death at Warrington.

A navvy named Martin Towey, aged sixty years. lodging at 14, Friars'green, was found dead at one o'clock on Sunday afternoon, under singular cir- cumstances. Deceased had retired to rest at mid- night on Saturday, and was then under the influ- ence of drink. He slept in an attic along with another man. About 1.30 on Sunday morning a fellow-lodger-named John Conway came downstairs, and saw the deceased in a sitting posture at the door of the landlady's bedroom. Thinking be was agleep,Conway took no further notice of him. About one o'clock deceased was found in the same place by the landlady, and he was then dead. At the in- quest, which was held on Monday, Dr. Smith, who had made a post-mortem examination of the boiy, said he found that almost all the internal organs were diseased. Death was due to failure of the heart's action brought about by congestion of the lungs. The jury returned a verdict of "Death from natural causes."

Continued Progress.

At the Bank premises,Blittermarket-street, the annual meeting of the trustees and man tgers of the Warrington Savings Bank was held on Tuesday morning. Mr. William Long chairman of the Bard of Manavement presided, and there were algo present Mr John Geddes (vice-chairman), the Revs H V Pigot. THE REPORT.

Tho ACTUARY read the statement of accounts and the following report of the Committee of Management :—ln presenting the general statement of accounts of the bank for.the year ended 20th NovembPr,l9oo,the Committee of Management have much pleasure in drawing attention to the very satisfactory progress of the business of the bank. During the year 789 ccounta have been opened, making the total number of depositors 12,636. Twenty- four thousand nine hundred and two transactions were recorded as compared with 23XL last year, showing an increase of 1,094. The increase in the amount owing to deposit•ors in the same period is £7,546 18s 31, making the total amount now due to them £353,463 34 7d., in aidition to which there are invegtmentt, on their behalf to the extent of £9984 04 61. in stock. The assets of the bank are £359,224 193 id., showing a surplus of £5,761 151 61 after deducting the whole amount due to depositors. The National Debt Commissioners hold on behalf of the institution a sum of £357,924 194 ld, which is considerably in excess of the total liabilities. The extracted list of depositors' balances cat tifisd by the auditsr, is placed on the counter during Ithe hours of business, and any depositor may satisfy himself by its inspection, that the amount to ifs credit, in accordanca with his pass book, is certified to the Government. The bank is ope.n daily for the transaction of business, and SUM as low as one shilling are received Fifty pounds may be deposited in any one year until the amount to the credit of the depositor reaches £2OO. A further £2OO may also be received in any year for in- vestment in Government stock. Payments not exceed- ing £lO are made on demand, larger amounts are paid subject to a week's notice. The continued steady in- cre34e in the number of depositor,' accounts, and in the number of transactions, is a most gratifying feature of the w of the bank. The greater proportion of the transactions are of small amounts which shows thr.t the bank is taken advantage of by the class ..f depositors for whose benefit the institiation was estahllened in the rear 1818. There are 5,163 depositors whose accounts do not exceed one pound. THE CHAIRMAN'S REMARKS. They brought their money to a bank I the managers of which they all know, and he kthe chaii- ' man) thought that and the publicity given to all their transactions had its weight with the public. Bearing upon that was the surplus, which was gradually rising over and above all their liabilities, of more than £5,000 (Flear, bear.) The work of the bank had gone on steadily, and the best evidence of the bank's success was that there was no very startling or sensational report to lay bEf Ire the meeting. THE WAR.

LATEST TELEGRAMS.

The Invasion of Cape

Colony.

Despatch from Lord Kitchener.

Mounted Troops sent against Them.

The War Office has issued the following : From Lord Kitchener to the Secretary of State for War :

Pretoria, Dccember 19, 8.15 p.m.—A party of Boers, estimated at 500 to 800, has crossed the Orange River at Rhenoster Hoek, and a second bend is reported to have crossed near Sanddrift. They have been followed, and I have sent down a considerable body of mounted men, who are getting around theta.

The Boers from Rhenoster Hoek were followed closely to Venterstadt, which they left at 3 p.m. yesterday, going towards Steynsburg.

The important points on tie railway in this neighbourhood are well guarded, and I hope these bands will soon be driven S rth again.

===== 0002601_19001221_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
Boers Occupying Colesberg Districi..

Cape Town, Dscember 20.—The invasion of the colony is spreading. The Boers are occupying Colesberg district, and pope are much disturbed there.

Personal.

We understand that the Tramways Committee of the Salford Corporation will recommend the Salford Town Council at their next meeting to appoint Mr Hatton, tramways engineer in Salford, as tram- ways engineer and general manager of the new electric tramways system. At present the salary of Mr Hatton is £240 per year, and he is expected to receive a substantial advance in his new position Mr Hatton who was articled with Messrs Pearson and Knowles, Bewsey and Dallam Forges, lived at Minim Farm. His appointment as been received with satisfaction by his many friends in this district.

Local Teachers in Conferen2e.

A wall-attended general meeting OC-the local association of the National Union of Teachers was held in the Virinmarleigh C 4f4 on laturday aft-omoon, the president, Mr S Jeffrey, in the chair. The nominations of officials of the Union for the coming year occupied first attention. Dr Beach, of Maccles. field. was nominated vice-president, and Mr Hamilton was re-nominated treasurer, while the support of the association was accorded to the candidature for the executive of Mr J Wright., of Liverpool. and Mr Sharpies, of Manchester, Mr J Upson, honorary secretary and treasurer, was elected representative to attend the annual Easter Conference, which will be held at Yarmouth.—Mr. Stansfield moved the following resolution :—" That the time has now come when it is desirable that this Association of Warrington Teachers should be recogaiz id by the authorities in this town in all matters concerning education, and should be represented on those bodies having to do with educational affairs." Mr StanQfleld re- ferred to the excellent wGrk being done in many towns by teacher members of various educational committees, and contended that being actively en- gaged in educational work they were well qualified to deal with such matters, and as practical man often knew where the shoe pinched, He referred more especially to the Papil Teachers' Classes Com- mittee, and urged that the association ought to be represented thereon by its own nominees.—Ar ROBO seconded the resolution, which was passed without dissent.—At the conclusion of the meeting six new members were nominated. Subsequently an excellent tea was provided by sirs Hayward. The remainder of the evening was devoted to dancing and music, which appeared to give pleasure to all present. Songs were rendered effictively by Mrs A Brown, the Misses Woodward, Molyneux, and Moores, and Megirs Evans, Wright, Molyneux, and Denison, whilst the following accompanied on the pianoforte or played for dancing : qrs Dennison, Mrs colledge, Misses Miller, Gornall, Ditchfield, Moly- neux. and Wright, and Messrs Blackwell, Denni- son,.Molyneux, Brown, and Parker.—At the con- clusion the president proposed, and the secretary seconded a hearty vote of thanks to those who had so kindly contributed to the evening's enjoyment, which was passed with acclamation.

AT CHRISTMAS TIME. You are puzzled what to give your friends and neighbours. You want to give them something good, something useful, some- thing that will be a pleasant reminder of your thoughtfulness. Now, there is no present more welcome in a household than a canister of really good tea. It does not spoil if not used immediately, and is never out of season. There is, too, a peculiar appropriateness in a present of tea being made at Christmas when each home has its festival. But when you buy, be sure you get good tea, tea that will make a friend of every lady who drinks it, a tea like Brooke, Bond's Tea, a small spoonful of which is stronger and better than a large spoonful of other tea. On Monday the ill- tineesa.ehTohr aslimcdhsemofisTtesstLmenotniaolrs f Testimonials. stamp's by and - nese continued and plaintiff asked her mother to go to prietors. LINCOLN AND MIDLAND COUNTIES DRUG Mr. Hickman and tell him she could not go to COMPANY LINCOLN. Beware of worthless imtations. work that day. The mother did not go to Mr Hickman's until 5.30. Defendant's son asked her if she had not received a letter which he sent, and she replied that she had not. It appeared that the letter came during her absence asking plaintiff to consider her engagement at an end. It made it very awkward for him for plaintiff to stop away without giving him notice. Plaintiff went to work on Tuesday morning and spoke to Mr Hickman about re-engaging her. He said he would consult his son and told her she had better go home and stay there until she had eot the neuralgia out of her system. Sub- sequently he wrote a letter stating.that his deciaion was final and he could not re-engage her. Defendant claimed for two weeks wages at 94 per week. Defendant had also deducted 4s from her wages for three bank holidays and she also sued for that amount. Plaintiff was called and gave evidence bearing out the above statement. Cress-examined by Mr Forshaw, she admitted that she went to the Cemetery on Sunday afternoon. She had not received previous complaints from defendant about her conduct. Mrs Harvey, the plaintiff's mother,said that on Fri Alee and Saturday nights her daughter waOldistracted with neuralgia. On Sunday morning she was very ill but in the afternoon she felt a little better and went to the Cemetery. On Monday she was unable to go to work and witness saw Mr Hickman at 5.30 p.m. For the defence, William Hickman, the defendant was called and stated that the plaintiff had been in his ser- vice for 13 months. It was her duty to be at the shop by 8.30 in the morning and remain there until 8 in the evening, meal times being allowed. She had neglected her duties several times and he had complained to her about it and also about coming late in the morn- ing. On one occasion be went to the shop at 915 and she had not arrived then. In answer to his Honour witness admitted that he raised the plaintiff's wages three weeks ago. The neglig- ence which he complained about occurred before he gave her the advance. Continuing he said that the girl told him she had toothache and he said he could give her a remedy that would cure toothache in an instant. His Honour: If you could do that you could make a fortune in a fortnight. (Laughter.) That is what we have been wanting for 2,000 years. (Laughter.) Continuing wiSness said that in consequence of plain- tiff not going to work on Monday morning he was un• I able to conduct a sale which had been arranged, as he had to remain en the shop. He had to see several persons and during his absence the shop had to be closed. Be estimated a loss of £l. on the sale and Es on havi ngtto close the shop. When the plaintiff came on Tuesday she seemed to be better and witness questioned whether be had been ill. He could scarcely believe what she aid. His Honour: So far as I have heard I believe every- thing she has said. Frank Hickman, the defendant's son corroborated. Mr Forshaw submitted that the plaintiff had been ne- glectful in her duties. It seemed to him that it was strange the girl should be out on Sunday afternoon if she was sufferieg from toothache. He contended that it was certainly her duty, if she was ill, to have let Mr Hickman know at the earliest possible moment, She had not worked one of the weeks they were claiming for and further she had frequently been absent from her business, and had been warned and cautioned. He sub- mitted that the defendent was doing perfectly right in dismiesing her. At the most she was only entitled to one week's wages. His Honour : I have every admiration for a good man struggling with adversity as you are hare. Con- tinuing he said the affected disbelief by the defendant did him very little credit. The suggestion as to her commencing work late in the morning was entirely dis- posed of by him raising her wages within three weeks of the event. It was impossible for him to suppose that they could not go on with the sale owing to her absence. He dismissed the counter claim and gave judgment for the plaintiff with full costa.
